# Cold War

This article is about the state of political tension in the 20th century. For the general term, see [Cold war (term)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_war_\(term\)). For other uses, see [Cold War (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War_\(disambiguation\)).

"Cold Warrior" redirects here. For other uses, see [Cold Warrior (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_Warrior_\(disambiguation\)).

The **Cold War** was a period of international [geopolitical](163225.md) rivalry between the [United States](3434750.md) (US) and the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) (USSR) and their respective allies, the [capitalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalism) [Western Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Bloc) and [communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism) [Eastern Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc). It began in [the aftermath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aftermath_of_World_War_II) of the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War)[^cite_note-duration-2] and ended with the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union) in 1991. The term *[cold war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_war_\(term\))* is used because there was no direct fighting between the two [superpowers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superpower), though each supported opposing sides in [regional conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_conflicts_related_to_the_Cold_War) known as [proxy wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proxy_war). In addition to the struggle for ideological and economic influence and an [arms race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arms_race) in both conventional and [nuclear weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_arms_race), the Cold War was expressed through technological rivalries such as the [Space Race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Race), [espionage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War_espionage), [propaganda campaigns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propaganda_campaign), [embargoes](411315.md), and [sports diplomacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_diplomacy).

After the end of the Second World War in 1945, during which the US and USSR had been allies, the USSR installed [satellite governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_state) in [its occupied territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_occupations_by_the_Soviet_Union) in Eastern Europe and [North Korea](21255.md) by 1949, resulting in the political division of Europe (and [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Germany_\(1945%E2%80%931990\))) by an "[Iron Curtain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Curtain)". The USSR tested [its first nuclear weapon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RDS-1) in 1949, four years after the US used its own [on Hiroshima and Nagasaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_bombings_of_Hiroshima_and_Nagasaki), and allied with the [People's Republic of China](5405.md), [founded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China) in 1949. The US declared the [Truman Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truman_Doctrine) of "[containment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Containment)" of communism in 1947, launched the [Marshall Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Plan) in 1948 to assist Western Europe's economic recovery, and founded the [NATO](21133.md) [military alliance](250766.md) in 1949 (matched by the Soviet-led [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact) in 1955). The [Berlin Blockade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Blockade) of 1948 to 1949 was an early confrontation, as was the [Korean War](16772.md) of 1950 to 1953, which ended in a stalemate.

[US involvement in regime change during the Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_involvement_in_regime_change_during_the_Cold_War) included support for [First World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_World) [anti-communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communism) and [right-wing dictatorships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right-wing_dictatorship) and uprisings, while [Soviet involvement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_involvement_in_regime_change) included the funding of [Second World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World) [left-wing parties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left-wing_parties), [wars of independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wars_of_national_liberation), and dictatorships. As nearly all the colonial states underwent [decolonization](302084.md), many became [Third World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_World) battlefields of the Cold War. Both powers used economic aid in an attempt to win the loyalty of [non-aligned countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement). The [Cuban Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Revolution) of 1959 installed the first communist regime in the Western Hemisphere, and in 1962, the [Cuban Missile Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Missile_Crisis) began after deployments of US missiles in Europe and Soviet missiles in Cuba; it is widely considered [the closest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_nuclear_close_calls) the Cold War came to escalating into [nuclear war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_warfare). Another major proxy conflict was the [Vietnam War](32611.md) of 1955 to 1975, which ended in a North Vietnamese victory.

The USSR solidified its domination of Eastern Europe with its crushing of the [Hungarian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_Revolution_of_1956) in 1956 and the [Warsaw Pact invasion of Czechoslovakia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact_invasion_of_Czechoslovakia) in 1968. Relations between the USSR and China broke down by 1961, with the [Sino-Soviet split](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Soviet_split) bringing the two states to the brink of war amid [a border conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Soviet_border_conflict) in 1969. In 1972, [the US initiated diplomatic contacts with China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_visit_by_Richard_Nixon_to_China) and the US and USSR signed [a series of treaties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_nuclear_technology_treaties) limiting their nuclear arsenals during a period known as [détente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%A9tente). In 1979, the toppling of US-allied governments in [Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_revolution) and [Nicaragua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicaraguan_Revolution) and the outbreak of the [Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War) again raised tensions. In 1985, [Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev) became [leader of the USSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_leaders_of_the_Soviet_Union) and [expanded political freedoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_secretaryship_of_Mikhail_Gorbachev), which contributed to the [revolutions of 1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutions_of_1989) in the Eastern Bloc and the collapse of the USSR in 1991, ending the Cold War.

## Terminology

Main article: [Cold war (term)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_war_\(term\))

Writer [George Orwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Orwell) used *[cold war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_war_\(term\))*, as a general term, in his essay "You and the Atomic Bomb", published 19 October 1945. Contemplating a world living in the shadow of the threat of [nuclear warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_warfare), Orwell looked at [James Burnham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Burnham)'s predictions of a polarized world, writing:

> Looking at the world as a whole, the drift for many decades has been not towards anarchy but towards the reimposition of slavery... James Burnham's theory has been much discussed, but few people have yet considered its ideological implications—that is, the kind of world-view, the kind of beliefs, and the social structure that would probably prevail in a state which was at once unconquerable and in a permanent state of "cold war" with its neighbours.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOrwell1945-3]

In *[The Observer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Observer)* of 10 March 1946, Orwell wrote, "after the Moscow conference last December, Russia began to make a 'cold war' on Britain and the British Empire."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOrwell1946-4]

The first use of the term to describe the specific [post-war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-war) geopolitical confrontation between the Soviet Union and the United States came in a speech by [Bernard Baruch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernard_Baruch), an influential advisor to Democratic presidents,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis200554-5] on 16 April 1947. The speech, written by journalist [Herbert Bayard Swope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbert_Bayard_Swope),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESafire2006-6] proclaimed, "we are today in the midst of a cold war."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGlass2016-7] Newspaper columnist [Walter Lippmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Lippmann) gave the term wide currency with his book *The Cold War*. When asked in 1947 about the source of the term, Lippmann traced it to a French term from the 1930s, *la guerre froide*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETalbott2009441_n._3-8][^cite_note-9]

## Background

Main article: [Origins of the Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origins_of_the_Cold_War)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of the Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Cold_War).

Animosity between the [Allies of World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II) predated the end of the war. The death of [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt) in April 1945 and the changeover to [Harry Truman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_S._Truman)'s presidential administration led to changes in US-Soviet relations as Truman and his staff began to take a harder diplomatic line. US and Soviet diplomats disputed conflicting interpretations of agreements made between Stalin and Roosevelt at the [Yalta Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yalta_Conference). Truman's administration was divided on the question of the Polish government's composition, with Truman and several cabinet members advocating for stronger demands that broader democratic elements be included in its ranks.[^cite_note-\:0-10]<sup>: 30 </sup> A 1944 accord between Churchill and Stalin further complicated matters in Eastern Europe; the two had agreed to non-interference in one another's spheres of influence, in Greece and in Romania and Bulgaria respectively.[^cite_note-\:0-10]<sup>: 28 </sup> The creation of the United Nations in the spring of 1945 led to further disagreements over how the postwar international order should be governed. Because of the alignment of many Latin American nations with the United States, the Soviets feared that a pro-American majority would be used against them. They opposed the admission of Argentina into the UN, and called for a more expansive [Security Council veto](1900272.md).[^cite_note-\:0-10]<sup>: 35–39 </sup> Stalin ultimately accommodated the American positions on the UN veto and on the Polish government, allowing [Oskar Lange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oskar_R._Lange) and [Stanisław Mikołajczyk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanis%C5%82aw_Miko%C5%82ajczyk) to join it.[^cite_note-\:0-10]<sup>: 47 </sup> The abrupt cancellation of the [lend-lease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lend-Lease) in May[^cite_note-\:0-10]<sup>: 43 </sup> and the August [atomic bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_bombings_of_Hiroshima_and_Nagasaki) in Japan, which took place just days before a planned Soviet entry into the [Pacific war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_War), further increased tensions.[^cite_note-\:0-10]<sup>: 58 </sup>

## Containment, Truman Doctrine, Korean War (1947–1953)

Main articles: [Cold War (1947–1948)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War_\(1947%E2%80%931948\)), [Cold War (1948–1953)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War_\(1948%E2%80%931953\)), [Soviet empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_empire), [Containment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Containment), and [Truman Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truman_Doctrine)

### Iron Curtain, Iran, Turkey, Greece, and Poland

Further information: [X Article](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X_Article), [Iron Curtain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Curtain), [Iran crisis of 1946](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran_crisis_of_1946), [Greek Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Civil_War), and [Restatement of Policy on Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restatement_of_Policy_on_Germany)



Remains of the "Iron Curtain" in the [Czech Republic](5321.md), 2014

In February 1946, [George F. Kennan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_F._Kennan)'s "[Long Telegram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X_Article)" from Moscow to Washington helped to articulate the US government's increasingly hard line against the Soviets, which would become the basis for US strategy toward the Soviet Union. The telegram galvanized a policy debate that would eventually shape the [Truman administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Harry_S._Truman)'s Soviet policy.[^cite_note-11] Washington's opposition to the Soviets accumulated after broken promises by Stalin and [Molotov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vyacheslav_Molotov) concerning Europe and Iran.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasanli2014221–222-12] Following the World War II [Anglo-Soviet invasion of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Soviet_invasion_of_Iran), the country was occupied by the Red Army in the far north and the British in the south.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESebestyen2014-13] Iran was used by the United States and British to supply the Soviet Union, and the Allies agreed to withdraw from Iran within six months after the cessation of hostilities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESebestyen2014-13] However, when this deadline came, the Soviets remained in Iran under the guise of the [Azerbaijan People's Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijan_People's_Government) and [Kurdish](35032860.md) [Republic of Mahabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Mahabad).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKinzer200365–66-14] On 5 March, former British prime minister Winston Churchill delivered his famous "[Iron Curtain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Curtain)" speech ([Fulton Speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fulton_Speech)) calling for an Anglo-American alliance against the Soviets, whom he accused of establishing an "iron curtain" dividing Europe.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchmitz1999-15][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarriman1987–1988-16]

A week later, on 13 March, Stalin responded vigorously to the speech, saying Churchill could be compared to [Adolf Hitler](2731583.md) insofar as he advocated the racial superiority of [English-speaking nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_territories_where_English_is_an_official_language) so that they could satisfy their hunger for world domination, and that such a declaration was "a call for war on the USSR." The Soviet leader also dismissed the accusation that the USSR was exerting increasing control over the countries lying in its sphere. He argued that there was nothing surprising in "the fact that the Soviet Union, anxious for its future safety, \[was] trying to see to it that governments loyal in their attitude to the Soviet Union should exist in these countries."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCauley2008143-17][^cite_note-18]



European military alliances



European economic blocs

Soviet territorial demands to Turkey regarding the Dardanelles in the [Turkish Straits crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_Straits_crisis) and Black Sea [border disputes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_territorial_claims_against_Turkey) were also a major factor in increasing tensions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasanli2014221–222-12][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoberts2011-19] In September, the Soviet side produced the [Novikov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolai_Vasilevich_Novikov) telegram, sent by the Soviet ambassador to the US but commissioned and "co-authored" by [Vyacheslav Molotov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vyacheslav_Molotov); it portrayed the US as being in the grip of monopoly capitalists who were building up military capability "to prepare the conditions for winning world supremacy in a new war".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKydd2018107-20] On 6 September 1946, [James F. Byrnes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_F._Byrnes) delivered a [speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restatement_of_Policy_on_Germany) in Germany repudiating the [Morgenthau Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morgenthau_Plan) (a proposal to partition and de-industrialize post-war Germany) and warning the Soviets that the US intended to maintain a military presence in Europe indefinitely.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis200530-21][^cite_note-22] As Byrnes stated a month later, "The nub of our program was to win the German people ... it was a battle between us and Russia over minds ..." In December, the Soviets agreed to withdraw from Iran after persistent US pressure, an early success of containment policy.

By 1947, US president [Harry S. Truman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_S._Truman) was outraged by the perceived resistance of the Soviet Union to American demands in Iran, Turkey, and Greece, as well as Soviet rejection of the [Baruch Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baruch_Plan) on nuclear weapons.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMilestones:_1945–1952-23] In February 1947, the British government announced that it could no longer afford to finance the [Kingdom of Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Greece) against the [Communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KKE)-led [DSE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Army_of_Greece) in the [Greek Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Civil_War).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIatrides1996373–376-24] In the same month, Stalin conducted the rigged [1947 Polish legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1947_Polish_legislative_election) which constituted an open breach of the [Yalta Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yalta_Agreement). The [US government](195149.md) responded by adopting a policy of [containment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Containment),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis200528–29-25] with the goal of stopping the spread of [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism). Truman delivered a speech calling for the allocation of \$400 million to intervene in the war and unveiled the [Truman Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truman_Doctrine), which framed the conflict as a contest between free peoples and [totalitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totalitarian) regimes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis200528–29-25] American policymakers accused the Soviet Union of conspiring against the Greek royalists in an effort to [expand Soviet influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domino_theory) even though Stalin had told the Communist Party to cooperate with the British-backed government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerolymatos2017195–204-26][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber1993194–197-27][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis200538-28]

Enunciation of the Truman Doctrine marked the beginning of a US bipartisan defense and foreign policy consensus between [Republicans](32070.md) and [Democrats](5043544.md) focused on containment and [deterrence](735661.md) that weakened during and after the [Vietnam War](32611.md), but ultimately persisted thereafter.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPaterson198935,_142,_212-29] Moderate and conservative parties in Europe, as well as social democrats, gave virtually unconditional support to the Western alliance,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoschonas200221-30] while [European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurocommunism) and [American Communists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_USA), financed by the [KGB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KGB) and involved in its intelligence operations,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrewMitrokhin2000276-31] adhered to Moscow's line, although dissent began to appear after 1956. Other critiques of the consensus policy came from [anti-Vietnam War activists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposition_to_United_States_involvement_in_the_Vietnam_War), the [Campaign for Nuclear Disarmament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campaign_for_Nuclear_Disarmament), and the [anti-nuclear movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-nuclear_movement).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrockerHampsonAall200755-32]

### Marshall Plan, Czechoslovak coup and formation of two German states

Main articles: [Marshall Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Plan), [Western Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Bloc), and [1948 Czechoslovak coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Czechoslovak_coup_d'%C3%A9tat)



The labeling used on the [Marshall Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Plan) economic [aid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aid) to Western Europe



Map of Cold War-era Europe and the [Near East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_East) showing countries that received Marshall Plan aid. The red columns show the relative amount of total aid received per nation.



Construction in [West Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Berlin) under Marshall Plan aid

In early 1947, France, Britain and the United States unsuccessfully attempted to reach an agreement with the Soviet Union for a plan envisioning an economically self-sufficient Germany, including a detailed accounting of the industrial plants, goods and infrastructure already taken by the Soviets.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller200016-33] In June 1947, in accordance with the [Truman Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truman_Doctrine), the United States enacted the [Marshall Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Plan), a pledge of economic assistance for all European countries willing to participate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller200016-33] Under the plan, which President Harry S. Truman signed on 3 April 1948, the US government gave to Western European countries over \$13 billion (equivalent to \$189 billion in 2016). Later, the program led to the creation of the [OECD](33853117.md).

The plan's aim was to rebuild the democratic and economic systems of Europe and to counter perceived threats to the [European balance of power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_balance_of_power), such as communist parties seizing control.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis1990186-34] The plan also stated that European prosperity was contingent upon German economic recovery.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDinan201740-35] One month later, Truman signed the [National Security Act of 1947](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Act_of_1947), creating a unified [Department of Defense](7279897.md), the [Central Intelligence Agency](5183633.md) (CIA), and the [National Security Council](44026.md) (NSC). These would become the main bureaucracies for US defense policy in the Cold War.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell1999916-36]

Stalin believed economic integration with the West would allow [Eastern Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc) countries to escape Soviet control, and that the US was trying to buy a pro-US re-alignment of Europe.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis200532-37] Stalin therefore prevented Eastern Bloc nations from receiving Marshall Plan aid.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis200532-37] The Soviet Union's alternative to the Marshall Plan, which was purported to involve Soviet subsidies and trade with central and eastern Europe, became known as the [Molotov Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molotov_Plan) (later institutionalized in January 1949 as the [Council for Mutual Economic Assistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comecon)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber1993194–197-27] Stalin was also fearful of a reconstituted Germany; his vision of a post-war Germany did not include the ability to rearm or pose any kind of threat to the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005105–106-38]

In early 1948, Czech Communists executed a [coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Czechoslovak_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) in [Czechoslovakia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Czechoslovak_Republic) (resulting in the formation of the [Czechoslovak Socialist Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Czechoslovak_Socialist_Republic)), the only Eastern Bloc state that the Soviets had permitted to retain democratic structures.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWettig200886-39] The public brutality of the coup shocked Western powers more than any event up to that point and swept away the last vestiges of opposition to the Marshall Plan in the [United States Congress](31756.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller200019-40][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrenville2005370–371-41]

In an immediate aftermath of the crisis, the [London Six-Power Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Six-Power_Conference) was held, resulting in the [Soviet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) boycott of the [Allied Control Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allied_Control_Council) and its incapacitation, an event marking the beginning of the full-blown Cold War, as well as ending any hopes at the time for a single German government and leading to formation in 1949 of the [Federal Republic of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Germany) and [German Democratic Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Germany).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWettig200896–100-42]

The twin policies of the Truman Doctrine and the Marshall Plan led to billions in economic and military aid for Western Europe, Greece, and Turkey. With the US assistance, the Greek military [won its civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Civil_War).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell1999916-36] Under the leadership of [Alcide De Gasperi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcide_De_Gasperi) the Italian [Christian Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Democracy_\(Italy\)) defeated the powerful [Communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Communist_Party)–[Socialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Socialist_Party) alliance in the [elections of 1948](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Italian_general_election),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005162-43] with covert support from the United States.[^cite_note-callanan41-44]<sup>: 41–45 </sup>

Outside of Europe, the United States also began to express interest in the development of many other countries, so that they would not fall under the sway of Eastern Bloc communism. In his January 1949 inaugural address, Truman declared for the first time in US history that [international development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_development) would be a key part of US foreign policy. The resulting program later became known as the [Point Four Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Point_Four_Program) because it was the fourth point raised in his address.[^cite_note-Hamilton_Page_57-45]

### Espionage

Main articles: [Cold War espionage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War_espionage), [American espionage in the Soviet Union and Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_espionage_in_the_Soviet_Union_and_Russian_Federation), and [Soviet espionage in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_espionage_in_the_United_States)

All major powers engaged in espionage, using a great variety of spies, [double agents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_agent), [moles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mole_\(espionage\)), and new technologies such as the tapping of telephone cables.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarthoff2004-46] The Soviet [KGB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KGB) ("Committee for State Security"), the bureau responsible for foreign espionage and internal surveillance, was famous for its effectiveness. The most famous Soviet operation involved its [atomic spies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_spies) that delivered crucial information from the United States' [Manhattan Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manhattan_Project), leading the USSR to detonate its first nuclear weapon in 1949, four years after the American detonation and much sooner than expected.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrewMitrokhin1999\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_August_2024\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (August_2024)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-47][^cite_note-48] A massive network of informants throughout the Soviet Union was used to monitor dissent from official Soviet politics and morals.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarthoff2004-46][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHopkins2007-49] Although to an extent [disinformation](142690.md) had always existed, the term itself was invented, and the strategy formalized by a [black propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_propaganda) department of the Soviet KGB.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor2016-50][^cite_note-51]

Based on the amount of top-secret Cold War archival information that has been released, historian [Raymond L. Garthoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raymond_L._Garthoff) concludes there probably was parity in the quantity and quality of secret information obtained by each side. However, the Soviets probably had an advantage in terms of [HUMINT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_intelligence_\(intelligence_gathering\)) (human intelligence or interpersonal espionage) and "sometimes in its reach into high policy circles." In terms of decisive impact, however, he concludes:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarthoff200429–30-52]

> We also can now have high confidence in the judgment that there were no successful "moles" at the political decision-making level on either side. Similarly, there is no evidence, on either side, of any major political or military decision that was prematurely discovered through espionage and thwarted by the other side. There also is no evidence of any major political or military decision that was crucially influenced (much less generated) by an agent of the other side.

According to historian Robert L. Benson, "Washington's forte was ['signals' intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Signals_intelligence) – the procurement and analysis of coded foreign messages," leading to the [Venona project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venona_project) or Venona intercepts, which monitored the communications of Soviet intelligence agents.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBensonWarner1996vii,_xix-53] [Moynihan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Patrick_Moynihan) wrote that the Venona project contained "overwhelming proof of the activities of Soviet spy networks in America, complete with names, dates, places, and deeds."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoynihan199815–16-54] The Venona project was kept highly secret even from policymakers until the [Moynihan Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moynihan_Commission_on_Government_Secrecy) in 1995.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoynihan199815–16-54] Despite this, the decryption project had already been betrayed and dispatched to the USSR by [Kim Philby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Philby) and [Bill Weisband](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Weisband) in 1946,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoynihan199815–16-54][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWest2002-55] as was discovered by the US by 1950.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBensonWarner1996xxvii,_xxviii-56] Nonetheless, the Soviets had to keep their discovery of the program secret, too, and continued leaking their own information, some of which was still useful to the American program.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWest2002-55] According to Moynihan, even President Truman may not have been fully informed of Venona, which may have left him unaware of the extent of Soviet espionage.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoynihan199870-57][^cite_note-trumanfas-58]

Clandestine [atomic spies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_spies) from the Soviet Union, who infiltrated the [Manhattan Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manhattan_Project) during WWII, played a major role in increasing tensions that led to the Cold War.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBensonWarner1996vii,_xix-53]

In addition to usual espionage, the Western agencies paid special attention to debriefing [Eastern Bloc defectors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emigration_from_the_Eastern_Bloc).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECowley1996157-59] [Edward Jay Epstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Jay_Epstein) describes that the CIA understood that the KGB used "provocations", or fake defections, as a trick to embarrass Western intelligence and establish Soviet double agents. As a result, from 1959 to 1973, the CIA required that East Bloc defectors went through a counterintelligence investigation before being recruited as a source of intelligence.[^cite_note-60]

During the late 1970s and 1980s, the KGB perfected its use of espionage to sway and distort diplomacy.[^cite_note-61] [Active measures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Active_measures) were "clandestine operations designed to further Soviet foreign policy goals," consisting of disinformation, forgeries, leaks to foreign media, and the channeling of aid to militant groups.[^cite_note-62] Retired KGB Major General [Oleg Kalugin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oleg_Kalugin) described active measures as "the heart and soul of [Soviet intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_historical_secret_police_organizations#Soviet_Union)."[^cite_note-63]

During the [Sino-Soviet split](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Soviet_split), "spy wars" also occurred between the USSR and PRC.[^cite_note-64]

### Cominform and the Tito–Stalin Split

Main articles: [Cominform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cominform) and [Tito–Stalin Split](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tito%E2%80%93Stalin_Split)

In September 1947, the Soviets created [Cominform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cominform) to impose orthodoxy within the international communist movement and tighten political control over Soviet [satellites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_state#Soviet_satellite_states) through coordination of communist parties in the [Eastern Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis200532-37] Cominform faced an embarrassing setback the following June, when the [Tito–Stalin split](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tito%E2%80%93Stalin_split) obliged its members to expel [Yugoslavia](34244.md), which remained communist but adopted a [non-aligned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement) position and began accepting financial aid from the US.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPapathanasiou201766-65]

Besides Berlin, the status of the city of [Trieste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trieste) was at issue. Until the break between Tito and Stalin, the Western powers and the Eastern bloc faced each other uncompromisingly. In addition to capitalism and communism, Italians and Slovenes, monarchists and republicans as well as war winners and losers often faced each other irreconcilably. The neutral [buffer state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buffer_state) [Free Territory of Trieste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Territory_of_Trieste), founded in 1947 with the United Nations, was split up and dissolved in 1954 and 1975, also because of the détente between the West and Tito.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJennings2017244-66][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERuzicic-Kessler2014-67]

### Berlin Blockade

Main article: [Berlin Blockade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Blockade)

&#x20;The US and Britain merged their western German occupation zones into "[Bizone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bizone)" (1 January 1947, later "Trizone" with the addition of France's zone, April 1949).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller200013-68] As part of the economic rebuilding of Germany, in early 1948, representatives of a number of Western European governments and the United States announced an agreement for a merger of western German areas into a federal governmental system.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller200018-69] In addition, in accordance with the [Marshall Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Plan), they began to re-industrialize and rebuild the West German economy, including the introduction of a new [Deutsche Mark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deutsche_Mark) currency to replace the old [Reichsmark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reichsmark) currency that the Soviets had debased.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller200031-70] The US had secretly decided that a unified and neutral Germany was undesirable, with [Walter Bedell Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Bedell_Smith) telling General Eisenhower "in spite of our announced position, we really do not want nor intend to accept German unification on any terms that the Russians might agree to, even though they seem to meet most of our requirements."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELayne200767-71]



American C-47s unloading at the [Berlin Tempelhof Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Tempelhof_Airport) during the Berlin Blockade

Shortly thereafter, Stalin instituted the Berlin Blockade (June 1948 – May 1949), one of the first major crises of the Cold War, preventing Western supplies from reaching West Germany's enclave of [West Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Berlin).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis200533-72] The United States (primarily), Britain, France, Canada, Australia, New Zealand, and several other countries began the massive "Berlin airlift", supplying West Berlin with provisions despite Soviet threats.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller200065–70-73]

The Soviets mounted a public relations campaign against the policy change. Once again, the East Berlin communists attempted to disrupt the [Berlin municipal elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Blockade#December_elections),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller200013-68] which were held on 5 December 1948 and produced a turnout of 86% and an overwhelming victory for the non-communist parties.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETurner198729-74] The results effectively divided the city into East and West, the latter comprising US, British and French sectors. 300,000 Berliners demonstrated and urged the international airlift to continue,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFritsch-Bournazel1990143-75] and US Air Force pilot [Gail Halvorsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gail_Halvorsen) created "[Operation Vittles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Blockade#%22Operation_Little_Vittles%22)", which supplied candy to German children.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller200026-76] The Airlift was as much a logistical as a political and psychological success for the West; it firmly linked West Berlin to the United States.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDaum200811–13,_41-77] In May 1949, Stalin lifted the blockade.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis200534-78][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller2000180–181-79]

In 1952, Stalin repeatedly [proposed a plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stalin_Note) to unify East and West Germany under a single government chosen in elections supervised by the United Nations, if the new Germany were to stay out of Western military alliances, but this proposal was turned down by the Western powers. Some sources dispute the sincerity of the proposal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEvan_Dijk1996-80]

### Beginnings of NATO and Radio Free Europe

Main articles: [NATO](21133.md), [Radio Free Europe/Radio Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Free_Europe/Radio_Liberty), [Eastern Bloc media and propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc_media_and_propaganda), and [Propaganda in the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propaganda_in_the_Soviet_Union)



President Truman signs the [North Atlantic Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic_Treaty) with guests in the Oval Office

Britain, France, the United States, Canada, and eight other Western European countries signed the [North Atlantic Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Atlantic_Treaty) of April 1949, establishing the [North Atlantic Treaty Organization](21133.md) (NATO).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis200534-78] That August, the [first Soviet atomic device](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RDS-1) was detonated in [Semipalatinsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semey), [Kazakh SSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakh_Soviet_Socialist_Republic).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber1993194–197-27] Following Soviet refusals to participate in a German rebuilding effort set forth by western European countries in 1948,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller200018-69][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETurner198723-81] the US, Britain and France spearheaded the establishment of the [Federal Republic of Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Germany) from the [three Western zones of occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bizone#Trizonia,_end_of_inter-Allied_control,_and_formation_of_West_Germany) in April 1949.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBungert1994-82] The Soviet Union proclaimed [its zone of occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_occupation_zone_in_Germany) in Germany the [German Democratic Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Germany) that October.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEByrd2003-83]

Media in the [Eastern Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc) was an [organ of the state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc_media_and_propaganda), completely reliant on and subservient to the communist party. Radio and television organizations were state-owned, while print media was usually owned by political organizations, mostly by the local communist party.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Neil199715–25-84] Soviet radio broadcasts used Marxist rhetoric to attack capitalism, emphasizing themes of labor exploitation, imperialism and war-mongering.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWood1992105-85]

Along with the broadcasts of the [BBC](19344654.md) and the [Voice of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voice_of_America) to Central and Eastern Europe,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPuddington2003131-86] a major propaganda effort began in 1949 was [Radio Free Europe/Radio Liberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Free_Europe/Radio_Liberty), dedicated to bringing about the peaceful demise of the communist system in the Eastern Bloc.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPuddington20039-87] Radio Free Europe attempted to achieve these goals by serving as a surrogate home radio station, an alternative to the controlled and party-dominated domestic press in the Soviet Bloc.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPuddington20039-87] Radio Free Europe was a product of some of the most prominent architects of America's early Cold War strategy, especially those who believed that the Cold War would eventually be fought by political rather than military means, such as George F. Kennan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPuddington20037-88] Soviet and Eastern Bloc authorities used various methods to suppress Western broadcasts, including [radio jamming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_jamming).[^cite_note-89][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBamford2003-90]

American policymakers, including Kennan and [John Foster Dulles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Foster_Dulles), acknowledged that the Cold War was in its essence a war of ideas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPuddington20037-88] The United States, acting through the CIA, funded a long list of projects to counter the communist appeal among intellectuals in Europe and the developing world.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPuddington200310-91] The CIA also [covertly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secrecy) sponsored a domestic propaganda campaign called [Crusade for Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusade_for_Freedom).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECummings2010-92]

### German rearmament

Main article: [West German rearmament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_German_rearmament)



Generals [Adolf Heusinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolf_Heusinger) and [Hans Speidel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_Speidel) sworn into the newly founded *[Bundeswehr](288188.md)* by [Theodor Blank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodor_Blank) in November 1955

The rearmament of West Germany was achieved in the early 1950s. Its main promoter was [Konrad Adenauer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konrad_Adenauer), the chancellor of West Germany, with France the main opponent. Washington had the decisive voice. It was strongly supported by the Pentagon (the US military leadership), and weakly opposed by President Truman; the State Department was ambivalent. The outbreak of the Korean War in June 1950 changed the calculations and Washington now gave full support. That also involved naming [Dwight D. Eisenhower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight_D._Eisenhower) in charge of NATO forces and sending more American troops to West Germany. There was a strong promise that West Germany would not develop nuclear weapons.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeisner2006356–374-93]

Widespread fears of another rise of [German militarism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Militarism#Germany) necessitated the new military to operate within an alliance framework under [NATO](21133.md) command.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESnyder2002-94] In 1955, Washington secured full German membership of NATO.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEByrd2003-83] In May 1953, [Lavrentiy Beria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lavrentiy_Beria), by then in a government post, had made an unsuccessful proposal to allow the reunification of a neutral Germany to prevent West Germany's incorporation into NATO, but his attempts were cut short after he was [executed several months later](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lavrentiy_Beria#Arrest,_trial_and_execution) during a Soviet power struggle.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005105-95] The events led to the establishment of the *[Bundeswehr](288188.md)*, the West German military, in 1955.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELarge1996\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_August_2024\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (August_2024)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-96][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHershberg1992-97]

### Chinese Civil War, SEATO, and NSC 68

Main article: [Cold War in Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War_in_Asia)



[Mao Zedong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mao_Zedong) and [Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin) in Moscow, December 1949

In 1949, [Mao Zedong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mao_Zedong)'s [People's Liberation Army](66890.md) defeated [Chiang Kai-shek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiang_Kai-shek)'s United States-backed [Kuomintang](16903.md) (KMT) [Nationalist Government in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_the_Republic_of_China). The KMT-controlled territory was now [restricted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuomintang's_retreat_to_Taiwan) to the island of [Taiwan](25734.md), the nationalist government of which exists to this day. The Kremlin promptly created an alliance with the newly formed [People's Republic of China](5405.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis200539-98] According to Norwegian historian [Odd Arne Westad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odd_Arne_Westad), the communists won the Civil War because they made fewer military mistakes than Chiang Kai-Shek made, and because in his search for a powerful centralized government, Chiang antagonized too many interest groups in China. Moreover, his party was weakened during the [war against Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Sino-Japanese_War). Meanwhile, the communists told different groups, such as the peasants, exactly what they wanted to hear, and they cloaked themselves under the cover of [Chinese nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_nationalism).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWestad2012291-99]

Confronted with the [communist revolution in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Communist_Revolution) and [the end of the American atomic monopoly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_atomic_bomb_project) in 1949, the Truman administration quickly moved to escalate and expand its [containment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Containment) doctrine.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber1993194–197-27] In [NSC 68](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NSC_68), a secret 1950 document, the National Security Council proposed reinforcing pro-Western alliance systems and quadrupling spending on defense.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber1993194–197-27] Truman, under the influence of advisor [Paul Nitze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Nitze), saw containment as implying complete [rollback](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rollback) of Soviet influence in all its forms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELayne200763–66-100]

United States officials moved to expand this version of containment into [Asia](689.md), [Africa](5334607.md), and [Latin America](18524.md), in order to counter revolutionary nationalist movements, often led by communist parties financed by the USSR.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005212-101] In this way, this US would exercise "[preponderant power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_projection)," oppose neutrality, and establish [global hegemony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superpower).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELayne200763–66-100] In the early 1950s (a period sometimes known as the "[Pactomania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pactomania)"), the US formalized a series of alliances with [Japan](15573.md) (a [former WWII enemy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan_during_World_War_II)), [South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Republic_of_Korea), [Taiwan](25734.md), [Australia](4689264.md), [New Zealand](4913064.md), [Thailand](30128.md), and the [Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Philippines_\(1946%E2%80%931965\)) (notably [ANZUS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ANZUS) in 1951 and [SEATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asia_Treaty_Organization) in 1954), thereby guaranteeing the United States a number of long-term military bases.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEByrd2003-83]

### Korean War

Main articles: [Division of Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_of_Korea), [Korean War](16772.md), and [Rollback](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rollback)



General [Douglas MacArthur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_MacArthur), UN Command CiC (seated), observes the naval shelling of [Incheon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incheon), Korea from [USS *Mt. McKinley*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Mount_McKinley), 15 September 1950

One of the more significant examples of the implementation of containment was the United Nations US-led intervention in the [Korean War](16772.md). In June 1950, after years of mutual hostilities,[^cite_note-102][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaruki20187–12-103][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStueck2013252–256-104] [Kim Il Sung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Il_Sung)'s [North Korean People's Army](21263.md) invaded South Korea. Stalin had been reluctant to support the invasion[^cite_note-105] but ultimately sent advisers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeathersby199328,_30-106] To Stalin's surprise,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber1993194–197-27] the [United Nations Security Council](31956.md) backed the defense of South Korea, although the Soviets were then [boycotting meetings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_boycott_of_the_United_Nations) in protest of the fact that [Taiwan](25734.md) (Republic of China), not the People's Republic of China, held a [permanent seat on the United Nations Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_members_of_the_United_Nations_Security_Council).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMalkasian200116-107] A [UN force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Command) of sixteen countries faced North Korea,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFehrenbach2001305-108] although 40 percent of troops were South Korean, and about 50 percent were from the United States.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECraigLogevall2012118-109]



[US Marines](17349325.md) engaged in street fighting during the liberation of [Seoul](19159283.md), September 1950

The US initially seemed to follow containment, only pushing back North Korea across the [38th Parallel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Division_of_Korea) and restoring South Korea's sovereignty while allowing North Korea's survival as a state. However, the success of the [Inchon landing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Inchon) inspired the US/UN forces to pursue a [rollback](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rollback) strategy instead and to overthrow communist North Korea, thereby allowing nationwide elections under UN auspices.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMatray1979-110] General [Douglas MacArthur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_MacArthur) then advanced into North Korea. The Chinese, fearful of a possible US invasion, [sent in a large army](16772.md#China_intervenes_\(October%E2%80%93December_1950\)) and pushed the UN forces back below the 38th parallel.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatersonCliffordBrighamDonoghue2014286–289-111] The episode was used to support the wisdom of the [containment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Containment) doctrine as opposed to rollback. The Communists were later pushed to roughly around the original border, with minimal changes. Among other effects, the Korean War galvanised NATO to develop a military structure.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIsbyKamps198513–14-112] The [Korean Armistice Agreement](30076555.md) was approved in July 1953.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOberdorfer200110–11-113][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENoOsterholm1996-114]

## Nuclear arms race and escalation (1953–1962)

Main article: [Cold War (1953–1962)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War_\(1953%E2%80%931962\))

### Khrushchev, Eisenhower, and de-Stalinization



NATO and Warsaw Pact troop strengths in Europe in 1959

In 1953, changes in political leadership on both sides shifted the dynamic of the Cold War.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell1999916-36] [Dwight D. Eisenhower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight_D._Eisenhower) was inaugurated president that January. During the last 18 months of the Truman administration, the American defense budget had quadrupled, and Eisenhower moved to reduce military spending by a third while continuing to fight the Cold War effectively.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber1993194–197-27]

Joseph Stalin [died in 1953](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Joseph_Stalin). [Nikita Khrushchev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikita_Khrushchev) eventually won the ensuing power struggle by the mid-1950s. In 1956, he [denounced Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Cult_of_Personality_and_Its_Consequences) and proceeded to ease controls over the party and society ([de-Stalinization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De-Stalinization)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell1999916-36]



From left to right: Soviet [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_heads_of_state_of_the_Soviet_Union) [Kliment Voroshilov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kliment_Voroshilov), Soviet [first secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Secretary_of_the_Communist_Party_of_the_Soviet_Union) [Nikita Khrushchev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikita_Khrushchev), and [Finnish president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Finland) [Urho Kekkonen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urho_Kekkonen) at Moscow in 1960

On 18 November 1956, while addressing Western dignitaries at a reception in Moscow's Polish embassy, Khrushchev infamously declared, "Whether you like it or not, history is on our side. [We will bury you](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/We_will_bury_you)", shocking everyone present.[^cite_note-115] He would later clarify he had not been referring to nuclear war, but the "historically fated victory of communism over capitalism."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis200584-116]

Eisenhower's secretary of state, John Foster Dulles, initiated a "[New Look](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Look_\(policy\))" for the [containment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Containment) strategy, calling for a greater reliance on nuclear weapons against US enemies in wartime.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell1999916-36] Dulles also enunciated the doctrine of "[massive retaliation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massive_retaliation)", threatening a severe US response to any Soviet aggression. Possessing nuclear superiority, for example, allowed Eisenhower to face down Soviet threats to intervene in the Middle East during the 1956 [Suez Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suez_Crisis).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber1993194–197-27] The declassified US plans for retaliatory nuclear strikes in the late 1950s included the "systematic destruction" of 1,200 major urban centers in the Soviet Bloc and China, including Moscow, East Berlin and Beijing.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBradner2015-117][^cite_note-118]

In spite of these events, there were substantial hopes for détente when [an upswing in diplomacy took place in 1959](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikita_Khrushchev#Early_relations_and_U.S._visit_\(1957%E2%80%931960\)), including a two-week visit by Khrushchev to the US, and plans for a two-power summit for May 1960. The latter was disturbed by the [U-2 spy plane scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_U-2_incident), however, in which Eisenhower was caught lying about the intrusion of American surveillance aircraft into Soviet territory.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchudson2015-119][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPatersonCliffordBrighamDonoghue2014306–308-120]

### Warsaw Pact and Hungarian Revolution

Main articles: [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact) and [Hungarian Revolution of 1956](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_Revolution_of_1956)

The Hungarian Revolution of 1956



March of protesters in Budapest, on 25 October;



A destroyed Soviet T-34-85 tank in Budapest

While [Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin)'s death in 1953 slightly relaxed tensions, the situation in Europe remained an uneasy armed truce.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhanna2013372-121] The Soviets, who had already created a network of mutual assistance treaties in the [Eastern Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc) by 1949, established a formal alliance therein, the [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact), in 1955. It stood opposed to NATO.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEByrd2003-83]



[Hungarian flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Hungary) (1949–1956) with the communist coat of arms cut out was an anti-Soviet revolutionary symbol

The [Hungarian Revolution of 1956](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_Revolution_of_1956) occurred shortly after Khrushchev arranged the removal of Hungary's Stalinist leader [Mátyás Rákosi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A1ty%C3%A1s_R%C3%A1kosi).[^cite_note-122] In response to a popular anti-communist uprising,[^cite_note-123] the new regime formally disbanded the [secret police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Protection_Authority), declared its intention to withdraw from the Warsaw Pact and pledged to re-establish free elections. The [Soviet Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Ground_Forces) invaded.[^cite_note-124] Thousands of Hungarians were killed and arrested, imprisoned and deported to the Soviet Union,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolodkov1956-125] and approximately 200,000 Hungarians fled Hungary.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECseresnyés199986–101-126] Hungarian leader [Imre Nagy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imre_Nagy) and others were executed following secret trials.[^cite_note-127]

From 1957 through 1961, Khrushchev openly and repeatedly threatened the West with nuclear annihilation. He claimed that Soviet missile capabilities were far superior to those of the United States, capable of wiping out any American or European city. According to [John Lewis Gaddis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Lewis_Gaddis), Khrushchev rejected Stalin's "belief in the inevitability of war," however. The new leader declared his ultimate goal was "[peaceful coexistence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peaceful_coexistence)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis200570-128] In Khrushchev's formulation, peace would allow capitalism to collapse on its own,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPerlmutter1997145-129] as well as giving the Soviets time to boost their military capabilities,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENjølstad2004136-130] which remained for decades until Gorbachev's later "new thinking" envisioning peaceful coexistence as an end in itself rather than a form of class struggle.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBreslauer200272-131]

The events in Hungary produced ideological fractures within the communist parties of the world, particularly in Western Europe, with great decline in membership, as many in both western and socialist countries felt disillusioned by the brutal Soviet response.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELendvai2008196-132] The communist parties in the West would never recover.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELendvai2008196-132]

### Rapacki Plan and Berlin Crisis of 1958–1959

Further information: [Rapacki Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapacki_Plan) and [Berlin Crisis of 1958–1959](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Crisis_of_1958%E2%80%931959)

In 1957, Polish foreign minister [Adam Rapacki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Rapacki) proposed the [Rapacki Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapacki_Plan) for a nuclear free zone in central Europe. Public opinion tended to be favourable in the West, but it was rejected by leaders of West Germany, Britain, France and the United States. They feared it would leave the powerful conventional armies of the Warsaw Pact dominant over the weaker NATO armies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStefancic1987-133]

During November 1958, Khrushchev made an unsuccessful attempt to turn all of Berlin into an independent, demilitarized "free city". He gave the United States, Great Britain and France a six-month ultimatum to withdraw their troops from the sectors of West Berlin, or he would transfer control of Western access rights to the East Germans. Khrushchev earlier explained to [Mao Zedong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mao_Zedong) that "Berlin is the testicles of the West. Every time I want to make the West scream, I squeeze on Berlin."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis200571-134] NATO formally rejected the ultimatum in mid-December and Khrushchev withdrew it in return for a Geneva conference on the German question.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaubman2004488–502-135]

### American military buildup

Main article: [Flexible response](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flexible_response)

Like Truman and Eisenhower, [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy) supported containment. President Eisenhower's [New Look](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Look_\(policy\)) policy had emphasized the use of less expensive nuclear weapons to [deter](735661.md) Soviet aggression by threatening massive nuclear attacks on all of the Soviet Union. Nuclear weapons were much cheaper than maintaining a large standing army, so Eisenhower cut conventional forces to save money. Kennedy implemented a new strategy known as [flexible response](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flexible_response). This strategy relied on conventional arms to achieve limited goals. As part of this policy, Kennedy expanded the [United States special operations forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_special_operations_forces), elite military units that could fight unconventionally in various conflicts. Kennedy hoped that the flexible response strategy would allow the US to counter Soviet influence without resorting to nuclear war.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHerring2008704–705-136]

To support his new strategy, Kennedy ordered a massive increase in defense spending and a rapid build-up of the nuclear arsenal to restore the lost superiority over the Soviet Union. In his inaugural address, Kennedy promised "to bear any burden" in the defense of liberty, and he repeatedly asked for increases in military spending and authorization of new weapons systems. From 1961 to 1964, the number of nuclear weapons increased by 50 percent, as did the number of B-52 bombers to deliver them. The new ICBM force grew from 63 intercontinental ballistic missiles to 424. He authorized 23 new Polaris submarines, each of which carried 16 nuclear missiles. Kennedy also called on cities to construct fallout shelters.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENash1993-137][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWarrenSiracusa2021-138]

### Competition in the Third World

Main articles: [Decolonization § After 1945](302084.md#After_1945), [Wars of national liberation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wars_of_national_liberation), [1953 Iranian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_Iranian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat), [1954 Guatemalan coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_Guatemalan_coup_d'%C3%A9tat), [Congo Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congo_Crisis), [1954 Geneva Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_Geneva_Conference), and [Bandung Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandung_Conference)

Economist [Adam Hanieh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Hanieh) argues that, in the post-WWII era, the global capitalist system was destabilized by a wave of decolonization movements that swept through Africa, Asia, and Latin America.[^cite_note-139] According to historian [L. S. Stavrianos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L._S._Stavrianos), inspired by the Soviet Union’s rapid socioeconomic progress, numerous revolutionary governments subsequently implemented state-planned economies and public ownership.[^cite_note-140] In his autobiography, the former Prime Minister of India, [Jawaharlal Nehru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru), proclaimed that "the presence and example of the Soviets was a bright and heartening phenomenon in a dark and dismal world."[^cite_note-141] Political scientist [Michael Parenti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Parenti) argues that these revolutionary governments implemented land reform and comprehensive programs for health, education, housing, and employment. By addressing the legacies of colonial exploitation and extreme poverty, he writes, these initiatives resulted in a historically unprecedented rise in the standard of living for hundreds of millions of people.[^cite_note-142] Economist [Samir Amin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samir_Amin) contends that these revolutionary governments also achieved progress in industrialization, economic equality, and the construction of modern states.[^cite_note-143] According to economist [Alice Amsden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alice_Amsden), World Bank data reveal a historic trend between 1950 and 1980: for the first time in recorded history, developing economies outpaced developed economies, achieving an average annual income growth of over 5% compared to 4% in the developed economies. She described this era as "a period of unprecedented expansion in living standards, [per capita income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Per_capita_income), wages, and poverty reduction."[^cite_note-144]

Political scientist [Michael Parenti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Parenti) argues that the U.S. launched a global "counter-revolution" to suppress the rise of revolutionary nationalist governments in the [Third World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_World), involving direct military interventions,[^cite_note-145] coups, funding opposition movements, and the training of military forces.[^cite_note-146] Historian Dov H. Levin notes that, between 1946 and 2000, the U.S. interfered in the elections of 81 countries.[^cite_note-147] According to [Mark Curtis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Curtis_\(British_author\)), the U.S. and other NATO allies actively backed fundamentalist Islamist movements throughout the Middle East, North Africa,[^cite_note-148][^cite_note-149] and Central and Southern Asia.[^cite_note-150][^cite_note-151] These movements were seen as a strategic buffer against Soviet influence and a counterforce to nationalist and socialist movements.[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154] This policy was formalized at the highest levels during the [Arab Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Cold_War), when, in 1957, President [Eisenhower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight_D._Eisenhower) approved a policy to "do everything possible to stress the 'holy war' aspect."[^cite_note-155] Reflecting on this policy decades later, Saudi Crown Prince [Mohammed bin Salman](38689358.md) said in 2018 that the [international spread of Saudi Wahhabism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_propagation_of_the_Salafi_movement_and_Wahhabism) was "rooted in the Cold War, when allies asked Saudi Arabia to use its resources to prevent inroads in Muslim countries by the Soviet Union."[^cite_note-156]

&#x20;Nationalist movements in some countries and regions, notably [Guatemala](17238567.md), Indonesia and [Indochina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainland_Southeast_Asia), were often allied with Communist groups or otherwise perceived to be unfriendly to Western interests.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell1999916-36] In this context, the United States and the Soviet Union increasingly competed for influence by proxy in the Third World as [decolonization](302084.md) gained momentum in the 1950s and early 1960s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005121–124-157] Both sides were selling armaments to gain influence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETowle2000160-158] The Kremlin saw continuing territorial losses by imperial powers as presaging the eventual victory of their ideology.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETucker20101566-159]



European [colonial empires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_empire) in Asia and Africa all collapsed in the years after 1945

The United States used the [Central Intelligence Agency](5183633.md) (CIA) to undermine neutral or hostile Third World governments and to support allied ones.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell199964,_916-160] In 1953, President Eisenhower implemented [Operation Ajax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_Iranian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat#Execution_of_Operation_Ajax), a covert coup operation to overthrow the Iranian prime minister, [Mohammad Mosaddegh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_Mosaddegh). The popularly elected Mosaddegh had been a Middle Eastern nemesis of Britain since nationalizing the British-owned [Anglo-Iranian Oil Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Persian_Oil_Company) in 1951. [Winston Churchill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_Churchill) told the United States that Mosaddegh was "increasingly turning towards Communist influence."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGasiorowskiByrne2004125-161][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1953-162] The pro-Western [shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah), [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](20065598.md), assumed control as an [autocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocracy) monarch.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatson2002118-163] The shah's policies included banning the communist [Tudeh Party of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tudeh_Party_of_Iran), and general suppression of political dissent by [SAVAK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAVAK), the shah's domestic security and intelligence agency.

In Guatemala, a [banana republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banana_republic), the [1954 Guatemalan coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_Guatemalan_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) ousted the left-wing President [Jacobo Árbenz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobo_%C3%81rbenz) with material CIA support.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStone2010199,_256-164] The post-Arbenz government—a [military junta](146728.md) headed by [Carlos Castillo Armas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Castillo_Armas)—repealed a [progressive land reform law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decree_900), returned nationalized property belonging to the [United Fruit Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Fruit_Company), set up a [National Committee of Defense Against Communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Committee_of_Defense_Against_Communism), and decreed a [Preventive Penal Law Against Communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preventive_Penal_Law_Against_Communism) at the request of the United States.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBulmer-Thomas1987142-165]

The non-aligned Indonesian government of [Sukarno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukarno) was faced with a major threat to its legitimacy beginning in 1956 when several regional commanders began to demand autonomy from [Jakarta](16275.md). After mediation failed, Sukarno took action to remove the dissident commanders. In February 1958, dissident military commanders in Central Sumatra (Colonel [Ahmad Husein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Husein)) and North Sulawesi (Colonel Ventje Sumual) declared the [Revolutionary Government of the Republic of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Government_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia)-[Permesta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permesta) Movement aimed at overthrowing the Sukarno regime. They were joined by many civilian politicians from the [Masyumi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masyumi_Party), such as [Sjafruddin Prawiranegara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sjafruddin_Prawiranegara), who were opposed to the growing influence of the communist [Partai Komunis Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Indonesia). Due to their anti-communist rhetoric, the rebels received arms, funding, and other covert aid from the CIA until [Allen Lawrence Pope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allen_Lawrence_Pope), an American pilot, was shot down after a bombing raid on government-held [Ambon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambon,_Maluku) in April 1958. The central government responded by launching airborne and seaborne military invasions of rebel strongholds at [Padang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Padang) and [Manado](844858.md). By the end of 1958, the rebels were militarily defeated, and the last remaining rebel guerilla bands surrendered by August 1961.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoadnight2002-166]



1961 USSR stamp commemorating [Patrice Lumumba](49240.md), assassinated prime minister of the [Republic of the Congo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Congolese_Republic)

In the [Republic of the Congo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_the_Congo_\(L%C3%A9opoldville\)), also known as Congo-Léopoldville, newly independent from [Belgium](3343.md) since June 1960, the [Congo Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congo_Crisis) erupted on 5 July leading to the secession of the regions [Katanga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Katanga) and [South Kasai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Kasai). CIA-backed President [Joseph Kasa-Vubu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Kasa-Vubu) ordered the dismissal of the democratically elected Prime Minister [Patrice Lumumba](49240.md) and the Lumumba cabinet in September over massacres by the armed forces during the [invasion of South Kasai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_South_Kasai) and for involving Soviets in the country.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENzongola-Ntalaja2011108-167][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchraeder199457-168] Later the CIA-backed Colonel [Mobutu Sese Seko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mobutu_Sese_Seko) quickly mobilized his forces to seize power through a military coup d'état,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchraeder199457-168] and worked with Western intelligence agencies to imprison Lumumba and hand him over to Katangan authorities who executed him by firing squad.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENzongola-Ntalaja2011-169][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerard2015216–218-170]

In [British Guiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Guiana), the leftist [People's Progressive Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Progressive_Party/Civic) (PPP) candidate [Cheddi Jagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheddi_Jagan) won the position of chief minister in a colonially administered election in 1953 but was quickly forced to resign from power after Britain's suspension of the still-dependent nation's constitution.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERose200257-171] Embarrassed by the landslide electoral victory of Jagan's allegedly Marxist party, the British imprisoned the PPP's leadership and maneuvered the organization into a divisive rupture in 1955.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarsYoung2004xviii-172] Jagan again won the colonial elections in 1957 and 1961, despite Britain's shift to a reconsideration of its view of the left-wing Jagan as a Soviet-style communist at this time. The United States pressured the British to withhold [Guyana](17238691.md)'s independence until an alternative to Jagan could be identified, supported, and brought into office.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPalmer2010247–248-173] In [Malaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_of_Malaya), the British colonialists [suppressed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayan_Emergency) the communist anti-colonial rebellion.

The [civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_conflicts_in_Vietnam_\(1945%E2%80%931949\)) and the [colonial war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Indochina_War) in Vietnam became internationalized and intertwined with the global Cold War when communist [China](5405.md) and the Soviet Union recognized the [Democratic Republic of Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Vietnam) (North Vietnam), while the [United States](3434750.md) and other Western bloc countries recognized the [State of Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Vietnam) in 1950.[^cite_note-Asselin_2024-174]<sup>: 90–95 </sup> Following the watershed defeat by the communist [Viet Minh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viet_Minh) rebels at the [Battle of Dien Bien Phu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Dien_Bien_Phu), the French accepted a negotiated abandonment of their neo-colonial stake in Vietnam in 1954. On June 4, France granted full sovereignty to the anti-communist State of Vietnam, an independent country within the [French Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Union).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETurner197593-175] At the [Geneva Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_Geneva_Conference) in July, peace accords were signed, but they ultimately resulted in Vietnam being divided at the [17th parallel north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/17th_parallel_north) between the Communist Bloc–allied North Vietnam and the Western Bloc–allied [South Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Vietnam). Between 1954 and 1961, Eisenhower's United States sent economic aid and military advisers to strengthen South Vietnam's government against communist efforts to destabilize it.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber1993194–197-27] China provided increased economic aid to North Vietnam in 1967–1968.[^cite_note-176]

Many emerging nations of Asia, Africa, and Latin America rejected the pressure to choose sides in the East–West competition. In 1955, at the [Bandung Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandung_Conference) in Indonesia, dozens of Third World governments resolved to stay out of the Cold War.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005126-177] The consensus reached at Bandung culminated with the creation of the [Belgrade](55904.md)-headquartered [Non-Aligned Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement) in 1961.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell1999916-36] Meanwhile, Khrushchev broadened Moscow's policy to establish ties with [India](14533.md) and other key neutral states. Independence movements in the Third World transformed the post-war order into a more pluralistic world of decolonized African and Middle Eastern nations and of rising nationalism in Asia and Latin America.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber1993194–197-27]

### Sino-Soviet split

Main article: [Sino-Soviet split](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Soviet_split)



Map showing greatest territorial extent of the Soviet Union and its aligned states, 1960, after the [Cuban Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Revolution) of 1959 but before the official [Sino-Soviet split](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Soviet_split) of 1961 (total area: c. 35,000,000 km

2

)

[^cite_note-178]
