After 1956, the Sino-Soviet alliance began to break down. Mao had defended Stalin when Khrushchev criticized him in 1956 and treated the new Soviet leader as a superficial upstart, accusing him of having lost his revolutionary edge.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005142-179] For his part, Khrushchev, disturbed by Mao's glib attitude toward nuclear war, referred to the Chinese leader as a "lunatic on a throne".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKempe201142-180]

After this, Khrushchev made many desperate attempts to reconstitute the Sino-Soviet alliance, but Mao considered it useless and denied any proposal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005142-179] The Chinese-Soviet animosity spilled out in an intra-communist propaganda war.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELüthi2010273–276-181] Further on, the Soviets focused on a bitter rivalry with Mao's China for leadership of the global communist movement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005140–142-182] Historian Lorenz M. Lüthi argues:

The Sino-Soviet split was one of the key events of the Cold War, equal in importance to the construction of the Berlin Wall, the Cuban Missile Crisis, the Second Vietnam War, and [Sino-American rapprochement](277880.md#Rapprochement). The split helped to determine the framework for the [Cold War period 1979–1985](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War_\(1979%E2%80%931985\)) in general, and influenced the course of the Second Vietnam War in particular.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELüthi20101-183]

### Space Race

Main article: [Space Race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Race)



Clockwise from top left: Sputnik 1, [Apollo 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_11) Moon landing, Space station [Mir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mir)

On the [nuclear weapons](21785.md) front, the United States and the Soviet Union pursued nuclear rearmament and developed long-range weapons with which they could strike the territory of the other.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEByrd2003-83] In August 1957, the Soviets successfully launched the world's first [intercontinental ballistic missile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercontinental_ballistic_missile) (ICBM),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcMahon200375–76-184] and in October they launched the first Earth satellite, [Sputnik 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sputnik_1).[^cite_note-185] This led to what became known as the [Sputnik crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sputnik_crisis). The [Central Intelligence Agency](5183633.md) described the orbit of Sputnik 1 as a "stupendous scientific achievement" and concluded that the USSR had likely perfected an intercontinental ballistic missile (ICBM) capable of reaching 'any desired target with accuracy'.[^cite_note-186]

The launch of Sputnik inaugurated the [Space Race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Race). This led to a series of historic space exploration milestones, and most notably the [Apollo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_program) [Moon landings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_landing) from 1969 by the United States, which astronaut [Frank Borman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Borman) later described as "just a battle in the Cold War."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKlesius2008-187] The public's reaction in the Soviet Union was mixed. The Soviet government limited the release of information about the lunar landing, which affected the reaction. A portion of the populace did not give it any attention, and another portion was angered by it.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDas2009-188] A major Cold War element of the Space Race was [satellite reconnaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconnaissance_satellite), as well as [signals intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Signals_intelligence) to gauge which aspects of the space programs had military capabilities.[^cite_note-189] The Soviet [Salyut programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salyut_programme), conducted in the 1970s and 1980s, put a crewed space station in long term orbit; two of the successful installations to the station were covers for secret military [Almaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaz) reconnaissance stations: [Salyut 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salyut_3) and [Salyut 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salyut_5).[^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191][^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193]

During the whole duration of the cold war, the US and the USSR represented the largest and dominant space powers of the world.[^cite_note-194] Despite their fierce competition, both nations signed international space treaties in the 1960s which would limit the militarization of space.[^cite_note-195]

The first research of [anti-satellite weapon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-satellite_weapon) technology also came about during this period.[^cite_note-196]

Later, the US and USSR pursued some cooperation in space as part of [détente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%A9tente), notably the [Apollo–Soyuz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo%E2%80%93Soyuz) orbital rendezvous and docking.[^cite_note-197]

### Aftermath of the Cuban Revolution

Main articles: [Consolidation of the Cuban Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consolidation_of_the_Cuban_Revolution) and [Bay of Pigs Invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bay_of_Pigs_Invasion)

In [Cuba](5042481.md), the [26th of July Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/26th_of_July_Movement), led by young revolutionaries [Fidel Castro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Castro) and [Che Guevara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Che_Guevara), seized power in the [Cuban Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Revolution) on 1 January 1959.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlumberg199523–24-198] Although Fidel Castro's first refused to categorize his new government as socialist and repeatedly denying being a communist, Castro appointed Marxists to senior government and military positions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBourne1986181–183-199][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEQuirk1993248–252-200][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEColtman2003162-201]



[Che Guevara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Che_Guevara) (left) and [Fidel Castro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Castro) (right) in 1961

[Diplomatic relations between Cuba and the United States](5231073.md) continued for some time after Batista's fall, but President Eisenhower deliberately left the capital to avoid meeting Castro during the latter's trip to [Washington, D.C.](108956.md) in April, leaving Vice President [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon) to conduct the meeting in his place.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELechuga_Hevia2001142-202] Cuba began negotiating for arms purchases from the Eastern Bloc in March 1960.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDominguez198922-203] The same month, Eisenhower gave approval to [CIA](5183633.md) plans and funding to overthrow Castro.[^cite_note-204]

In January 1961, just prior to leaving office, Eisenhower formally severed relations with the Cuban government. That April, the administration of newly elected American President [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy) mounted the unsuccessful CIA-organized [ship-borne invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bay_of_Pigs_Invasion) of the island by [Cuban exiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_exodus) at Playa Girón and Playa Larga in [Santa Clara Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Clara_Province)—a failure that publicly humiliated the United States.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199895-205] Castro responded by publicly embracing [Marxism–Leninism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marxism%E2%80%93Leninism), and the Soviet Union pledged to [provide further support](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuba%E2%80%93Soviet_Union_relations).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith199895-205] In December, the US government [began a violent campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Mongoose) of [terrorist](30636.md) attacks against civilians in Cuba, and [covert operations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covert_operations) and sabotage against the administration, in an attempt to overthrow the Cuban government.[^cite_note-212]

### Berlin Crisis of 1961

Main article: [Berlin Crisis of 1961](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Crisis_of_1961)

Further information: [Berlin Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Wall) and [Emigration from the Eastern Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emigration_from_the_Eastern_Bloc)



[Soviet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanks_of_the_Soviet_Union) and [American tanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanks_of_the_United_States) face each other at [Checkpoint Charlie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Checkpoint_Charlie) during the Berlin Crisis of 1961

The [Berlin Crisis of 1961](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Crisis_of_1961) was the last major incident in the Cold War regarding the status of Berlin and [post–World War II Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Germany_\(1945%E2%80%931990\)). By the early 1950s, the [Soviet approach to restricting emigration movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emigration_from_the_Eastern_Bloc) was emulated by most of the rest of the [Eastern Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDowty1989114-213] However, hundreds of thousands of [East Germans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Germany) annually emigrated to free and prosperous [West Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Germany) through a "loophole" in the system that existed between [East Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Berlin) and [West Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Berlin).[^cite_note-214][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarrison200399-215]

The emigration resulted in a massive "[brain drain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_capital_flight)" from East Germany to West Germany of younger educated professionals, such that nearly 20% of East Germany's population had migrated to West Germany by 1961.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDowty1989122-216] That June, the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) issued a new [ultimatum](198432.md) demanding the withdrawal of [Allied forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II) from West Berlin.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005114-217] The request was rebuffed, but the United States now limited its security guarantees to West Berlin.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDaum200827-218] On 13 August, East Germany erected a barbed-wire barrier that would eventually be expanded through construction into the [Berlin Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Wall), effectively closing the loophole and preventing its citizens from fleeing to the West.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPearson199875-219]

### Cuban Missile Crisis and Khrushchev's ousting

Main articles: [Operation Mongoose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Mongoose) and [Cuban Missile Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Missile_Crisis)

&#x20;The Kennedy administration continued seeking ways to oust Castro following the Bay of Pigs invasion, experimenting with various ways of covertly facilitating the overthrow of the Cuban government. Significant hopes were pinned on the program of terrorist attacks and other destabilization operations known as [Operation Mongoose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Mongoose), that was devised under the Kennedy administration in 1961. Khrushchev learned of the project in February 1962,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZubok1994-220] and preparations to install Soviet nuclear missiles in Cuba were undertaken in response.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZubok1994-220]



Aerial photograph of a Soviet missile site in [Cuba](5042481.md), taken by a US [spy aircraft](46818.md), 1 November 1962

Alarmed, Kennedy considered various reactions. He ultimately responded to the installation of nuclear missiles in Cuba with a [naval blockade](293054.md), and he presented an ultimatum to the Soviets. Khrushchev backed down from a confrontation, and the Soviet Union removed the missiles in return for a public American pledge not to invade Cuba again as well as a covert deal to remove US missiles from Turkey.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones,_H.2009122-221]

The [Cuban Missile Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Missile_Crisis) (October – November 1962) brought the world closer to [nuclear war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_warfare) than ever before.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis200582-222] The aftermath led to efforts in the [nuclear arms race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_arms_race) at nuclear disarmament and improving relations, although the Cold War's first arms control agreement, the [Antarctic Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antarctic_Treaty_System), had come into force in 1961.[^cite_note-223]

The compromise embarrassed Khrushchev and the Soviet Union because the withdrawal of US missiles from Italy and Turkey was a secret deal between Kennedy and Khrushchev, and the Soviets were seen as retreating from circumstances that they had started. In 1964, Khrushchev's Kremlin colleagues managed to [oust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikita_Khrushchev#Removal) him, but allowed him a peaceful retirement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005119–120-224] He was accused of rudeness and incompetence, and John Lewis Gaddis argues that he was also blamed with ruining Soviet agriculture, bringing the world to the brink of nuclear war, and becoming an "international embarrassment" when he authorized construction of the Berlin Wall.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005119-225] According to Dobrynin, the top Soviet leadership took the Cuban outcome as "a blow to its prestige bordering on humiliation".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaubman2004579-226][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENaftali2012-227]

## From confrontation to détente (1962–1979)

Main articles: [Cold War (1962–1979)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War_\(1962%E2%80%931979\)), [Closure of the Suez Canal (1967–1975)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Closure_of_the_Suez_Canal_\(1967%E2%80%931975\)), and [Era of Stagnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Era_of_Stagnation)

In the course of the 1960s and 1970s, Cold War participants struggled to adjust to a new, more complicated pattern of international relations in which the world was no longer divided into two clearly opposed blocs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell1999916-36] From the beginning of the post-war period with American help, Western Europe and Japan rapidly recovered from the destruction of World War II and sustained strong economic growth throughout the 1950s and 1960s, with per capita GDPs approaching those of the United States, while [Eastern Bloc economies stagnated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc#Economies).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell1999916-36][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHardtKaufman199516-228]



Soviet Premier [Alexei Kosygin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexei_Kosygin) with US President [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson) at the 1967 [Glassboro Summit Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glassboro_Summit_Conference)

In Indonesia, the hardline anti-communist [General Suharto](217513.md) wrested control from predecessor [Sukarno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukarno) in an attempt to [establish a "New Order"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transition_to_the_New_Order). From 1965 to 1966, with the [aid of the US](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA_activities_in_Indonesia#Anti-communist_purge) and other Western governments,[^cite_note-229][^cite_note-230][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThaler2015-231][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPerry2016-232][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBevins2017-233] the military [led the mass killing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_mass_killings_of_1965%E2%80%9366) of more than 500,000 members and sympathizers of the [Indonesian Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Indonesia) and other leftist organizations, and detained hundreds of thousands in prison camps under inhumane conditions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFarid20053–16-234][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAarons200780-235] A top-secret CIA report stated that the massacres "rank as one of the worst [mass murders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_murder) of the 20th century, along with the Soviet purges of the 1930s, the Nazi mass murders during the Second World War, and the Maoist bloodbath of the early 1950s."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAarons200780-235] These killings served US interests and constitute a major turning point in the Cold War as the balance of power shifted in Southeast Asia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBevins20202-236][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEScott2017-237]



[Suharto](217513.md) of Indonesia attending funeral of five generals slain in [30 September Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/30_September_Movement), 2 October 1965

The [Vietnam War](32611.md) descended into a quagmire for the United States, leading to a decline in international prestige and economic stability, derailing arms agreements, and provoking domestic unrest. America's withdrawal from the war led it to embrace a policy of [détente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%A9tente) with both China and the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMilestones:_1969–1976-238]



NATO and Warsaw Pact troop strengths in Europe in 1973

Backed by the [Kampuchean United Front for National Salvation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kampuchean_United_Front_for_National_Salvation), an organization of Khmer pro-Soviet Communists and Khmer Rouge defectors, Vietnam invaded Cambodia on 22 December 1978. The [invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodian%E2%80%93Vietnamese_War) succeeded in deposing Pol Pot, but the new state struggled to gain international recognition beyond the [Soviet Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc) sphere. Despite the international outcry at the Pol Pot regime's gross human rights violations, representatives of the [Khmer Rouge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmer_Rouge) were allowed to be seated in the [UN General Assembly](31957.md), with strong support from China, the Western powers, and the member countries of [ASEAN](106539.md). Following the destruction of the Khmer Rouge, the national reconstruction of Cambodia was hampered, and Vietnam suffered a punitive [Chinese attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Vietnamese_War).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESlocomb2001-239] Although unable to deter Vietnam from ousting Pol Pot, China demonstrated that its Cold War communist adversary, the Soviet Union, was unable to protect its Vietnamese ally.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEElleman2001\[httpsarchiveorgdetailsmodernchinesewar00ellepagen309_297\]-240] Former U.S. Secretary of State [Henry Kissinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Kissinger) wrote that "China succeeded in exposing the limits of...\[Soviet] strategic reach" and speculated that the desire to "compensate for their ineffectuality" contributed to the Soviets' decision to [intervene in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War) a year later.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKissinger2011304–305-241]

In the [1973 oil crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_oil_crisis), Organization of Petroleum Exporting Countries ([OPEC](166346.md)) cut their petroleum output. This raised oil prices and hurt Western economies, but helped the Soviet Union by generating a huge flow of money from its oil sales.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPainter2014-242]

As a result of the oil crisis, combined with the growing influence of Third World alignments, such as OPEC and the [Non-Aligned Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement), less powerful countries had more room to assert their independence and often showed themselves resistant to pressure from either superpower.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005212-101] Meanwhile, Moscow was forced to turn its attention inward to deal with the Soviet Union's deep-seated domestic economic problems.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell1999916-36] During this period, Soviet leaders such as [Leonid Brezhnev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonid_Brezhnev) and [Alexei Kosygin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexei_Kosygin) embraced the notion of détente.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell1999916-36]

### Vietnam War

Main articles: [Vietnam War](32611.md) and [Opposition to United States involvement in the Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposition_to_United_States_involvement_in_the_Vietnam_War)



US combat operations during the [Battle of Ia Drang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Ia_Drang), [South Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Vietnam), November 1965

Under President [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy), US troop levels in Vietnam grew from just under a thousand in 1959 to 16,000 in 1963.[^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244] South Vietnamese President Ngo Dinh Diem's heavy-handed [crackdown on Buddhist monks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu%E1%BA%BF_Ph%E1%BA%ADt_%C4%90%E1%BA%A3n_shootings) in 1963 led the US to endorse a deadly [military coup against Diem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1963_South_Vietnamese_coup).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillerWainstock2013315–325-245] The war escalated further in 1964 following the controversial [Gulf of Tonkin incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Tonkin_incident), in which a US destroyer was alleged to have clashed with North Vietnamese fast attack craft. The [Gulf of Tonkin Resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Tonkin_Resolution) gave President [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson) broad authorization to increase US military presence, deploying ground [combat units](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_organization) for the first time and increasing troop levels to 184,000.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKoven201593-246] Soviet leader Leonid Brezhnev responded by reversing Khrushchev's policy of disengagement and increasing aid to the North Vietnamese, hoping to entice the North from its pro-Chinese position. The USSR discouraged further escalation of the war, however, providing just enough military assistance to tie up American forces.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETucker2011131-247] From this point, the [People's Army of Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Army_of_Vietnam) (PAVN) engaged in more [conventional warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conventional_warfare) with US and South Vietnamese forces.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGlass2017-248]

The [Tet Offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tet_Offensive) of 1968 proved to be the turning point of the war. Despite years of American tutelage and aid, the South Vietnamese forces were unable to withstand the communist offensive and the task fell to US forces instead.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKalb2013-249] At the same time, in 1963–1965, American domestic politics saw the triumph of [liberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_liberalism_in_the_United_States). According to historian Joseph Crespino:

It has become a staple of twentieth-century historiography that Cold War concerns were at the root of a number of progressive political accomplishments in the postwar period: a high progressive marginal tax rate that helped fund the arms race and contributed to broad income equality; bipartisan support for far-reaching civil rights legislation that transformed politics and society in the American South, which had long given the lie to America's egalitarian ethos; bipartisan support for overturning an explicitly racist immigration system that had been in place since the 1920s; and free health care for the elderly and the poor, a partial fulfillment of one of the unaccomplished goals of the New Deal era. The list could go on.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrespino2020123-250]

### Nuclear testing and Use of Outer-Space treaties

The [Partial Nuclear Test Ban Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partial_Nuclear_Test_Ban_Treaty) was signed on August 5, 1963, by the United States, the Soviet Union, and over 100 other nations. This treaty banned nuclear weapons tests in the atmosphere, outer space, and underwater, restricting such tests to underground environments.[^cite_note-251][^cite_note-252][^cite_note-253][^cite_note-254] The treaty followed heightened concerns over the militarization of space, amplified by the United States' Starfish Prime test in 1962, which involved the detonation of a nuclear device in the upper atmosphere.[^cite_note-255][^cite_note-256]

To further delineate the peaceful use of outer space, the United Nations facilitated the drafting of the *[Treaty on Principles Governing the Activities of States in the Exploration and Use of Outer Space, including the Moon and Other Celestial Bodies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outer_Space_Treaty)*, commonly known as the Outer Space Treaty. Signed on January 27, 1967, by the United States, the Soviet Union, and the United Kingdom, it entered into force on October 10, 1967. The treaty established space as a domain to be used exclusively for peaceful purposes, prohibiting the placement of nuclear weapons or any other weapons of mass destruction in orbit or on celestial bodies.[^cite_note-257][^cite_note-258][^cite_note-259]

### Invasion of Czechoslovakia

Main articles: [Prague Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prague_Spring) and [Warsaw Pact invasion of Czechoslovakia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact_invasion_of_Czechoslovakia)



The [invasion of Czechoslovakia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact_invasion_of_Czechoslovakia) by the Soviet Union in 1968 was one of the biggest military operations on European soil since [World War II](32927.md)

In 1968, a period of political liberalization took place in [Czechoslovakia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Czechoslovak_Socialist_Republic) called the [Prague Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prague_Spring). An "[Action Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism_with_a_human_face)" of reforms included increasing [freedom of the press](105323.md), [freedom of speech](21401843.md) and [freedom of movement](1270497.md), along with an economic emphasis on [consumer goods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Final_good), the possibility of a multiparty government, limitations on the power of the secret police,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEllo196832,_54-260][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVon_GeldernSiegelbaum-261] and potential withdrawal from the Warsaw Pact.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005150-262]

In answer to the Prague Spring, on 20 August 1968, the [Soviet Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Ground_Forces), together with most of their Warsaw Pact allies, [invaded Czechoslovakia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact_invasion_of_Czechoslovakia).[^cite_note-263] The invasion was followed by a wave of emigration, including an estimated 70,000 Czechs and Slovaks initially fleeing, with the total eventually reaching 300,000.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEČulík1998-264][^cite_note-265] The invasion sparked intense protests from Yugoslavia, Romania, China, and from Western European countries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005154-266]

### Sino-Soviet split and Nixon-China visit



US President [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon) shakes hands with Chinese Premier [Zhou Enlai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhou_Enlai) at [Beijing Capital International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beijing_Capital_International_Airport)

As a result of the [Sino-Soviet split](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Soviet_split), tensions along the Chinese–Soviet border [reached their peak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Soviet_border_conflict) in 1969, when the Soviet planned to launch a [large-scale nuclear strike against China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Soviet_split#Planned_Soviet_nuclear_strike_on_China).[^cite_note-\:3-267] United States President [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon) intervened,[^cite_note-\:3-267] and decided to use the conflict to shift the balance of power towards the West in the Cold War through a policy of rapproachment with China, which began with his [1972 visit to China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_visit_by_Richard_Nixon_to_China) and culminated in 1979 with the signing of the [Joint Communiqué on the Establishment of Diplomatic Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Communiqu%C3%A9_on_the_Establishment_of_Diplomatic_Relations) by [President Carter and Chinese Communist Party leader Deng Xiaoping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visit_by_Deng_Xiaoping_to_the_United_States).[^cite_note-268][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005149–152-269]

### Nixon, Brezhnev, and détente

Main articles: [Presidency of Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Richard_Nixon), [Détente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%A9tente), [Brezhnev Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brezhnev_Doctrine), [Strategic Arms Limitation Talks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_Arms_Limitation_Talks), [Helsinki Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki_Accords), [Vladivostok Summit Meeting on Arms Control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladivostok_Summit_Meeting_on_Arms_Control), and [Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe](22796.md)

&#x20;Although indirect conflict between Cold War powers continued through the late 1960s and early 1970s, tensions were beginning to ease.[^cite_note-270] Following the ousting of Khrushchev, another period of [collective leadership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_leadership) ensued, consisting of Leonid Brezhnev as general secretary, [Alexei Kosygin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexei_Kosygin) as Premier and [Nikolai Podgorny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolai_Podgorny) as Chairman of the Presidium, lasting until Brezhnev established himself in the early 1970s as the preeminent Soviet leader.



[Nikolai Podgorny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolai_Podgorny) visiting [Tampere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tampere), Finland on 16 October 1969

Following his visit to China, Nixon met with Soviet leaders in Moscow.[^cite_note-271] These [Strategic Arms Limitation Talks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_Arms_Limitation_Talks) resulted in landmark arms control treaties. These aimed to limit the development of costly anti-ballistic missiles and nuclear missiles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarabell1999916-36]

Nixon and Brezhnev proclaimed a new era of "peaceful coexistence" and established the groundbreaking new policy of [détente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%A9tente) (or cooperation) between the superpowers. Meanwhile, Brezhnev attempted to revive the Soviet economy, which was declining in part because of heavy military expenditures. The Soviet Union's [military budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_budget) in the 1970s was massive, 40–60% of the federal budget and 15% of GDP.[^cite_note-272] Between 1972 and 1974, the two sides also agreed to strengthen their economic ties,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber1993194–197-27] including agreements for increased trade. As a result of their meetings, *détente* would replace the hostility of the Cold War and the two countries would live mutually.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELitwak1986-273] These developments coincided with [Bonn](3295.md)'s *[Ostpolitik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ostpolitik)* policy formulated by the West German Chancellor [Willy Brandt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Willy_Brandt),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005154-266] an effort to normalize relations between West Germany and Eastern Europe. Other agreements were concluded to stabilize the situation in Europe, culminating in the [Helsinki Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki_Accords) signed at the [Conference on Security and Co-operation in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conference_on_Security_and_Co-operation_in_Europe) in 1975.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005188-274]



Soviet general secretary [Leonid Brezhnev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonid_Brezhnev) and US President [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter) sign the [SALT II arms limitation treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_Arms_Limitation_Treaty_II) in Vienna on 18 June 1979

The Helsinki Accords, in which the Soviets promised to grant free elections in Europe, has been called a major concession to ensure peace by the Soviets. In practice, the Soviet government significantly curbed the [rule of law](25166191.md), [civil liberties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_liberties), [protection of law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criminal_justice), and [guarantees of property](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Property_rights),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPipes2001\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_August_2024\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (August_2024)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-275][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPipes1994401–403-276] which were considered examples of "bourgeois morality" by Soviet legal theorists such as [Andrey Vyshinsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrey_Vyshinsky).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyszyński1949153,_162-277] The Soviet Union signed legally-binding human rights documents, such as the [International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Covenant_on_Civil_and_Political_Rights) in 1973 and the Helsinki Accords in 1975, but they were neither widely known or accessible to people living under communist rule, nor were they taken seriously by the communist authorities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas2005117-278] Human rights activists in the Soviet Union were regularly subjected to harassment, repressions and arrests.

The pro-Soviet American business magnate [Armand Hammer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armand_Hammer) of [Occidental Petroleum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occidental_Petroleum) often mediated trade relations. Author [Daniel Yergin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Yergin), in his book *The Prize*, writes that Hammer "ended up as a go-between for five Soviet General Secretaries and seven US Presidents."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYergin2011557-279] Hammer had extensive business relationship in the Soviet Union stretching back to the 1920s with Lenin's approval.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCormick1980-280][^cite_note-\:8-281] According to *Christian Science Monitor* in 1980, "although his business dealings with the Soviet Union were cut short when Stalin came to power, he had more or less single-handedly laid the groundwork for the \[1980] state of Western trade with the Soviet Union."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCormick1980-280]

Kissinger and Nixon were "realists" who deemphasized idealistic goals like anti-communism or promotion of democracy worldwide because those goals were too expensive in terms of America's economic capabilities.[^cite_note-282] They rejected "idealism" as impractical and too expensive, and neither man showed much sensitivity to the plight of people living under communism. Kissinger's realism fell out of fashion as idealism returned to American foreign policy with Carter's moralism emphasizing human rights, and Reagan's rollback strategy aimed at destroying communism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchwartz2011-283]

### Late 1970s deterioration of relations

See also: [Soviet reaction to the Polish crisis of 1980–1981](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_reaction_to_the_Polish_crisis_of_1980%E2%80%931981), [Soviet invasion of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_invasion_of_Afghanistan), [Antisemitism in the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_in_the_Soviet_Union), and [Refuseniks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refuseniks)



Iranian people protesting against the [Pahlavi dynasty](338883.md), during the [Iranian Revolution](347268.md)

In the 1970s, the KGB, led by [Yuri Andropov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuri_Andropov), continued to persecute distinguished [Soviet dissidents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_dissidents), such as [Aleksandr Solzhenitsyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksandr_Solzhenitsyn) and [Andrei Sakharov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrei_Sakharov), who were criticising the Soviet leadership in harsh terms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005186-284] Indirect conflict between the superpowers continued through this period of détente in the Third World, particularly during political crises in the Middle East, Chile, Ethiopia, and Angola.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005178-285]

In 1973, Nixon announced his administration was committed to seeking [most favored nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Most_favoured_nation) trade status with the USSR,[^cite_note-286] which was challenged by Congress in the [Jackson-Vanik Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackson%E2%80%93Vanik_amendment).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHerring2008804-287] The United States had long linked trade with the Soviet Union to its foreign policy toward the Soviet Union and, especially since the early 1980s, to [Soviet human rights policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_the_Soviet_Union). The [Jackson-Vanik Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackson-Vanik_Amendment), which was attached to the [1974 Trade Act](5672090.md), linked the granting of [most-favored-nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Most-favored-nation) to the USSR to the right of persecuted [Soviet Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Jews) to emigrate. Because the Soviet Union refused the right of emigration to Jewish [refuseniks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refusenik), the ability of the President to apply most-favored nation trade status to the Soviet Union was restricted.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPomeranz2010-288]

Although President [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter) tried to place another limit on the arms race with a [SALT II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_Arms_Limitation_Talks#SALT_II_Treaty) agreement in 1979,[^cite_note-289] his efforts were undermined by the other events that year, including the [Iranian Revolution](347268.md) and the [Nicaraguan Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicaraguan_Revolution), which both ousted pro-US governments, and his retaliation against the [Soviet coup in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Storm-333) in December.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber1993194–197-27]

## Renewal of tensions (1979–1985)

Main article: [Cold War (1979–1985)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War_\(1979%E2%80%931985\))



Protest in Amsterdam against the deployment of [Pershing II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pershing_II) missiles in Europe, 1981

The period in the late 1970s and early 1980s showed an intensive reawakening of Cold War tensions and conflicts. Tensions greatly increased between the major powers with both sides becoming more militant.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalliday20012e-290] [Diggins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Patrick_Diggins) says, "Reagan went all out to fight the second cold war, by supporting counterinsurgencies in the third world."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDiggins2007267-291] [Cox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Cox_\(academic\)) says, "The intensity of this 'second' Cold War was as great as its duration was short."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECox199018-292]

### Soviet invasion of Afghanistan and end of détente

Main articles: [Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War), [Carter Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carter_Doctrine), [Foreign policy of the Ronald Reagan administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_the_Ronald_Reagan_administration), [Operation Cyclone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Cyclone), [Saur Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saur_Revolution), and [Afghan Civil War (1989–1992)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_Civil_War_\(1989%E2%80%931992\))



The Soviet invasion during [Operation Storm-333](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Storm-333) on 26 December 1979

In April 1978, the communist [People's Democratic Party of Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Democratic_Party_of_Afghanistan) (PDPA) seized power in [Afghanistan](737.md) in the [Saur Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saur_Revolution). Within months, opponents of the communist regime launched an uprising in eastern Afghanistan that quickly expanded into a [civil war](27576770.md) waged by guerrilla [mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujahideen) against government forces countrywide.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHussain2005108–109-293] The [Islamic Unity of Afghanistan Mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_mujahideen) insurgents received military training and weapons in neighboring [Pakistan](23235.md) and [China](5405.md),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStarr2004157–158-294][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKinsella1992-295] while the Soviet Union sent thousands of military advisers to support the PDPA government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHussain2005108–109-293] Meanwhile, increasing friction between the competing factions of the PDPA—the dominant [Khalq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalq) and the more moderate [Parcham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parcham)—resulted in the dismissal of Parchami cabinet members and the arrest of Parchami military officers under the pretext of a Parchami coup. By mid-1979, the United States had started a covert program to assist the mujahideen.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeher200468–69,_94-296][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETobin2020-297]

In September 1979, Khalqist President [Nur Muhammad Taraki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nur_Muhammad_Taraki) was assassinated in a coup within the PDPA orchestrated by fellow Khalq member [Hafizullah Amin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafizullah_Amin), who assumed the presidency. Distrusted by the Soviets, Amin was assassinated by Soviet special forces during [Operation Storm-333](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Storm-333) in December 1979. Afghan forces suffered losses during the Soviet operation; 30 Afghan palace guards and over 300 army guards were killed while another 150 were captured.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCauley2008b142-298] In the aftermath of the operation, a total of 1,700 Afghan soldiers who surrendered to Soviet forces were taken as prisoners,[^cite_note-299] and the Soviets installed [Babrak Karmal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babrak_Karmal), the leader of the PDPA's Parcham faction, as Amin's successor. Veterans of the Soviet Union's [Alpha Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpha_Group) have stated that Operation Storm-333 was one of the most successful in the unit's history. Documents released following the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union) in the 1990s revealed that the Soviet leadership believed Amin had secret contacts within the [American embassy in Kabul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embassy_of_the_United_States,_Kabul) and "was capable of reaching an agreement with the United States";[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECooley20028-300] however, allegations of Amin colluding with the Americans have been widely discredited.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlight201270-301][^cite_note-302][^cite_note-303] The PDBA was tasked to fill the vacuum and carried out a purge of Amin supporters. Soviet troops were deployed to put Afghanistan under Soviet control with Karmal in more substantial numbers, although the Soviet government did not expect to do most of the fighting in Afghanistan. As a result, however, the Soviets were now directly involved in what had been a domestic war in Afghanistan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKalinovsky201125–28-304]

Carter responded to the Soviet invasion by withdrawing the [SALT II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_Arms_Limitation_Talks#SALT_II_Treaty) treaty from ratification, imposing embargoes on grain and technology shipments to the USSR, and demanding a significant increase in military spending, and further announced the [boycott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Summer_Olympics_boycott) of the [1980 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Summer_Olympics) in Moscow, which was joined by 65 other nations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEToohey2007100-305][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEaton2016-306][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETreadaway1996-307] He described the Soviet incursion as "the most serious threat to the peace since the Second World War".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005211-308]

### Reagan and Thatcher

Further information: [Reagan Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reagan_Doctrine) and [Thatcherism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thatcherism)



President Reagan publicizes his support by meeting with [Afghan mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_mujahideen) leaders in the White House, 1983



President Reagan with Prime Minister Margaret Thatcher during a working luncheon at [Camp David](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camp_David), December 1984



The world map of military alliances in 1980

In January 1977, four years prior to becoming president, [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) bluntly stated, in a conversation with [Richard V. Allen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_V._Allen), his basic expectation in relation to the Cold War. "My idea of American policy toward the Soviet Union is simple, and some would say simplistic," he said. "It is this: We win and they lose."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllen2000-309] In 1980, Ronald Reagan won the [1980 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_United_States_presidential_election), vowing to increase military spending and confront the Soviets everywhere.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005189-310] Both Reagan and new British Prime Minister [Margaret Thatcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher) denounced the Soviet Union and its ideology. Reagan labeled the Soviet Union an "[evil empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evil_Empire_speech)" and predicted that communism would be left on the "[ash heap of history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ash_heap_of_history)," while Thatcher inculpated the Soviets as "bent on world dominance."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005197-311] In 1982, Reagan tried to cut off Moscow's access to hard currency by impeding its proposed gas line to Western Europe. It hurt the Soviet economy, but it also caused ill will among American allies in Europe who counted on that revenue. Reagan retreated on this issue.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsno2018281–304-312][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraebnerBurnsSiracusa200829–31-313]

By early 1985, Reagan's anti-communist position had developed into a stance known as the new [Reagan Doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reagan_Doctrine)—which, in addition to containment, formulated an additional right to subvert existing communist governments.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraebnerBurnsSiracusa200876-314] Besides continuing Carter's policy of supporting the Islamic opponents of the Soviet Union and the Soviet-backed [PDPA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Democratic_Party_of_Afghanistan) government in Afghanistan, the CIA also sought to weaken the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) itself by promoting [Islamism](15012.md) in the majority-Muslim [Central Asian Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Central_Asia).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESingh2005130-315] Additionally, the CIA encouraged anti-communist Pakistan's ISI to train Muslims from around the world to participate in the [jihad](16203.md) against the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESingh2005130-315]

### Polish Solidarity movement and martial law

Main articles: [Solidarity (Polish trade union)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solidarity_\(Polish_trade_union\)) and [Martial law in Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_law_in_Poland)

Further information: [Soviet reaction to the Polish crisis of 1980–1981](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_reaction_to_the_Polish_crisis_of_1980%E2%80%931981)

[Pope John Paul II](23805.md) provided a moral focus for [anti-communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communism); a visit to his native Poland in 1979 stimulated a religious and nationalist resurgence centered on the [Solidarity movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solidarity_\(Polish_trade_union\)) trade union that galvanized opposition, and may have led to his [attempted assassination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attempted_assassination_of_Pope_John_Paul_II) two years later.[^cite_note-316][^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318] In December 1981, Poland's [Wojciech Jaruzelski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wojciech_Jaruzelski) reacted to the crisis by imposing [a period of martial law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martial_law_in_Poland). Reagan imposed economic sanctions on [Poland](22936.md) in response.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005219–222-319] [Mikhail Suslov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Suslov), the Kremlin's top ideologist, advised Soviet leaders not to intervene if Poland fell under the control of Solidarity, for fear it might lead to heavy economic sanctions, resulting in a catastrophe for the Soviet economy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005219–222-319]

### US and USSR military and economic issues

Further information: [Era of Stagnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Era_of_Stagnation), [Strategic Defense Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_Defense_Initiative), [RSD-10 Pioneer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RSD-10_Pioneer), and [MGM-31 Pershing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MGM-31_Pershing)



US and USSR/Russian nuclear weapons stockpiles, 1945–2006

The Soviet Union had built up a military that consumed as much as 25 percent of its gross national product at the expense of [consumer goods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumer_goods_in_the_Soviet_Union) and investment in civilian sectors.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber2002332-320] Soviet spending on the [arms race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arms_race) and other Cold War commitments both caused and exacerbated deep-seated structural problems in the Soviet system,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETowle2000159-321] which experienced at least [a decade of economic stagnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Era_of_Stagnation) during the late Brezhnev years.

Soviet investment in the defense sector was not driven by military necessity but in large part by the interests of the [nomenklatura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomenklatura), which was dependent on the sector for their own power and privileges.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber2002335-322] The [Soviet Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Armed_Forces) became the largest in the world in terms of the numbers and types of weapons they possessed, in the number of troops in their ranks, and in the sheer size of their [military–industrial base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military%E2%80%93industrial_complex).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOdom20001-323] However, the quantitative advantages held by the Soviet military often concealed areas where the Eastern Bloc dramatically lagged behind the West.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber2002340-324] For example, the [Persian Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War) demonstrated how the [armor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vehicle_armour), [fire control systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire-control_system), and firing range of the Soviet Union's most common main battle tank, the [T-72](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-72), were drastically inferior to the American [M1 Abrams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M1_Abrams), yet the USSR fielded almost three times as many T-72s as the US deployed M1s.[^cite_note-325]



Delta 183 launch vehicle lifts off, carrying the [Strategic Defense Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_Defense_Initiative) sensor experiment "Delta Star"

By the early 1980s, the USSR had built up a military arsenal and army surpassing that of the United States. Soon after the Soviet invasion of Afghanistan, President Carter began massively building up the United States military. This buildup was accelerated by the Reagan administration, which increased the military spending from 5.3 percent of GNP in 1981 to 6.5 percent in 1986,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarlinerAlesina19916-326] the largest peacetime defense buildup in United States history.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeeney2006-327] The American-Soviet tensions present during 1983 was defined by some as the start of "Cold War II". While in retrospective this phase of the Cold War was generally defined as a "war of words",[^cite_note-328] the Soviet's "peace offensive" was largely rejected by the West.[^cite_note-329]

Tensions continued to intensify as Reagan revived the [B-1 Lancer](4952.md) program, which had been canceled by the Carter administration,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELee200813-330] produced [LGM-118 Peacekeeper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGM-118_Peacekeeper) missiles,[^cite_note-331] installed US cruise missiles in Europe, and announced the experimental [Strategic Defense Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_Defense_Initiative), dubbed "Star Wars" by the media, a defense program to shoot down missiles in mid-flight.[^cite_note-332] The Soviets deployed [RSD-10 Pioneer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RSD-10_Pioneer) [ballistic missiles](38025.md) targeting Western Europe, and NATO decided, under the impetus of the Carter presidency, to deploy [MGM-31 Pershing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MGM-31_Pershing) and cruise missiles in Europe, primarily West Germany.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005202-333] This deployment placed missiles just 10 minutes' striking distance from Moscow.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarthoff1994881–882-334]

After Reagan's military buildup, the Soviet Union did not respond by further building its military,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELebowStein1994-335] because the enormous military expenses, along with inefficient [planned manufacturing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_economy) and [collectivized agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collectivization_in_the_Soviet_Union), were already a heavy burden for the [Soviet economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_Soviet_Union).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllen2001-336] At the same time, [Saudi Arabia](349303.md) increased oil production,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaidar2007190–205-337] even as other non-OPEC nations were increasing production.[^cite_note-338] These developments contributed to the [1980s oil glut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980s_oil_glut), which affected the Soviet Union as oil was the main source of Soviet export revenues.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber2002332-320] Issues with [command economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planned_economy#Command_economy),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHardtKaufman19951-339] oil price decreases and large military expenditures gradually brought the Soviet economy to stagnation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaidar2007190–205-337]



After ten-year-old American [Samantha Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samantha_Smith) wrote a letter to [Yuri Andropov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuri_Andropov) expressing her fear of nuclear war, Andropov invited Smith to the Soviet Union

On 1 September 1983, the Soviet Union shot down [Korean Air Lines Flight 007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_Air_Lines_Flight_007), a [Boeing 747](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_747) with 269 people aboard, including sitting Congressman [Larry McDonald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_McDonald), an action which Reagan characterized as a massacre. The airliner was en route from Anchorage to Seoul but owing to a navigational mistake made by the crew, it flew through Russian [prohibited airspace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prohibited_airspace). The [Soviet Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Air_Force) treated the unidentified aircraft as an intruding U.S. [spy plane](46818.md) and destroyed it with [air-to-air missiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air-to-air_missiles).[^cite_note-tapes-340] The incident increased support for military deployment, overseen by Reagan, which stood in place until the later accords between Reagan and Mikhail Gorbachev.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETalbottHannifinMagnusonDoerner1983-341] During the early hours of 26 September 1983, the [1983 Soviet nuclear false alarm incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Soviet_nuclear_false_alarm_incident) occurred; systems in [Serpukhov-15](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serpukhov-15) underwent a glitch that claimed several [intercontinental ballistic missiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercontinental_ballistic_missile) were heading towards Russia, but officer [Stanislav Petrov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanislav_Petrov) correctly suspected it was a [false alarm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_alarm), ensuring the Soviets did not respond to the non-existent attack.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoffman1999-342] As such, he has been credited as "the man who saved the world".[^cite_note-343] The [Able Archer 83](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Able_Archer_83) exercise in November 1983, a realistic simulation of a coordinated NATO nuclear release, was perhaps the most dangerous moment since the Cuban Missile Crisis, as the Soviet leadership feared that a nuclear attack might be imminent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005228-344]

American domestic public concerns about intervening in foreign conflicts persisted from the end of the Vietnam War.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber2002323-345] The Reagan administration emphasized the use of quick, low-cost [counterinsurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterinsurgency) tactics to intervene in foreign conflicts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber2002323-345] In 1983, the Reagan administration intervened in the multisided [Lebanese Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_Civil_War), [invaded Grenada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_invasion_of_Grenada), [bombed Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_United_States_bombing_of_Libya) and backed the Central American [Contras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contras), anti-communist paramilitaries seeking to overthrow the Soviet-aligned [Sandinista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandinista_National_Liberation_Front) government in Nicaragua.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005212-101] While Reagan's interventions against Grenada and Libya were popular in the United States, his backing of the Contra rebels was [mired in controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Contra_affair).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEReagan1991-346] The Reagan administration's backing of the military government of [Guatemala](17238567.md) during the [Guatemalan Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guatemalan_Civil_War), in particular the regime of [Efraín Ríos Montt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efra%C3%ADn_R%C3%ADos_Montt), was also controversial.[^cite_note-347]

Meanwhile, the Soviets incurred high costs for their own foreign interventions. Although Brezhnev was convinced in 1979 that the [Soviet war in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War) would be brief, Muslim guerrillas, aided by the US, China, Britain, Saudi Arabia, and Pakistan,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKinsella1992-295] waged a fierce resistance against the invasion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber2002314-348] The Kremlin sent nearly 100,000 troops to support its puppet regime in Afghanistan, leading many outside observers to dub the war "the Soviets' Vietnam".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber2002314-348] However, Moscow's quagmire in Afghanistan was far more disastrous for the Soviets than Vietnam had been for the Americans because the conflict coincided with a period of internal decay and domestic crisis in the Soviet system.

A senior [US State Department](31975.md) official predicted such an outcome as early as 1980, positing that the invasion resulted in part from a:

> ...domestic crisis within the Soviet system. ... It may be that the thermodynamic law of [entropy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entropy) has ... caught up with the Soviet system, which now seems to expend more energy on simply maintaining its equilibrium than on improving itself. We could be seeing a period of foreign movement at a time of internal decay.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDobrynin2001438–439-349]

## Final years (1985–1991)

Main article: [Cold War (1985–1991)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War_\(1985%E2%80%931991\))

### Gorbachev's reforms

Further information: [Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev), [Perestroika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perestroika), and [Glasnost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasnost)



[Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev) in one-to-one discussions with US President [Ronald Reagan](25433.md)



Mikhail Gorbachev and Ronald Reagan sign the [INF Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intermediate-Range_Nuclear_Forces_Treaty) at the White House, 1987

By the time the comparatively youthful [Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev) became [General Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Secretary_of_the_Communist_Party_of_the_Soviet_Union) in 1985,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005197-311] the Soviet economy was stagnant and faced a sharp fall in foreign currency earnings as a result of the downward slide in oil prices in the 1980s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber2002331–333-350] These issues prompted Gorbachev to investigate measures to revive the ailing state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber2002331–333-350]

An ineffectual start led to the conclusion that deeper structural changes were necessary, and in June 1987 Gorbachev announced an agenda of economic reform called *[perestroika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perestroika)*, or restructuring.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005231–233-351] Perestroika relaxed the [production quota](22136400.md) system, allowed cooperative ownership of small businesses and paved the way for foreign investment. These measures were intended to redirect the country's resources from costly Cold War military commitments to more productive areas in the civilian sector.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005231–233-351]

Despite initial skepticism in the West, the new Soviet leader proved to be committed to reversing the Soviet Union's deteriorating economic condition instead of continuing the arms race with the West.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber2002300–340-352] Partly as a way to fight off internal opposition from party cliques to his reforms, Gorbachev simultaneously introduced *[glasnost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasnost)*, or openness, which increased freedom of the press and the transparency of state institutions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGibbs19997-353] *Glasnost* was intended to reduce the corruption at the top of the [Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_the_Soviet_Union) and moderate the [abuse of power](4308572.md) in the [Central Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Committee_of_the_Communist_Party_of_the_Soviet_Union).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGibbs199933-354] Glasnost also enabled increased contact between Soviet citizens and the Western world, particularly with the United States, contributing to the accelerating [détente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C3%A9tente) between the two nations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGibbs199961-355]

### Thaw in relations

Further information: [Reykjavík Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reykjav%C3%ADk_Summit), [Intermediate-Range Nuclear Forces Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intermediate-Range_Nuclear_Forces_Treaty), [START I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/START_I), and [Treaty on the Final Settlement with Respect to Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_on_the_Final_Settlement_with_Respect_to_Germany)



The beginning of the 1990s brought a thaw in relations between the superpowers

In response to the Kremlin's military and [political concessions](2538448.md), Reagan agreed to renew talks on economic issues and the scaling-back of the arms race.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005229–230-356] The first [summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Summit_\(1985\)) was held in November 1985 in [Geneva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva), Switzerland.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005229–230-356] A [second summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reykjav%C3%ADk_Summit) was held in October 1986 in [Reykjavík](25798.md), Iceland. Talks went well until the focus shifted to Reagan's proposed [Strategic Defense Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_Defense_Initiative) (SDI), which Gorbachev wanted to be eliminated. Reagan refused.[^cite_note-357] The negotiations failed, but the third summit ([Washington Summit (1987)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Summit_\(1987\)), 8–10 December 1987) led to a breakthrough with the signing of the [Intermediate-Range Nuclear Forces Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intermediate-Range_Nuclear_Forces_Treaty) (INF). The INF treaty eliminated all nuclear-armed, ground-launched ballistic and cruise missiles with ranges between 500 and 5,500 kilometers (310 and 3,420 mi) and their infrastructure.[^cite_note-358]



"[Tear down this wall!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tear_down_this_wall!)" speech: Reagan speaking in front of the [Brandenburg Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brandenburg_Gate), 12 June 1987

During 1988, it became apparent to the Soviets that oil and gas subsidies, along with the cost of maintaining massive troops levels, represented a substantial economic drain.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShearman199576-359] In addition, the security advantage of [a buffer zone](5188.md) was recognized as irrelevant and the Soviets [officially declared](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinatra_Doctrine) that they would no longer intervene in the affairs of [satellite states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact) in Central and Eastern Europe.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005248-360] [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush) and Gorbachev met at the [Moscow Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Summit_\(1988\)) in May 1988 and the [Governors Island Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governors_Island_Summit) in December 1988.

In 1989, [Soviet forces withdrew from Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_withdrawal_from_Afghanistan) without achieving their objectives.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005235–236-361] Later that year, the [Berlin Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Berlin_Wall), the [Inner German border](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inner_German_border#Fall_of_the_inner_German_border) and the [Iron Curtain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Curtain#Fall_of_the_Iron_Curtain) fell. On 3 December 1989, Gorbachev and Bush declared the Cold War over at the [Malta Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malta_Summit). In February 1990, Gorbachev agreed with the US-proposed [Treaty on the Final Settlement with Respect to Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_on_the_Final_Settlement_with_Respect_to_Germany) and signed it on 12 September 1990, paving the way for the [German reunification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_reunification).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShearman199576-359] When the Berlin Wall came down, Gorbachev's "[Common European Home](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_European_Home)" concept began to take shape.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEuropean_Navigator1989-362][^cite_note-363] The two former adversaries were partners in the [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War) against [Iraq](26215470.md) (August 1990 – February 1991).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMotin2025111–112-364][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman199341-365] During the final summit in Moscow in July 1991, Gorbachev and Bush signed the [START I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/START_I) arms control treaty.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005255-366]

### Eastern Europe breaks away

Main article: [Revolutions of 1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutions_of_1989)



[Otto von Habsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_von_Habsburg), who played a leading role in opening the [Iron Curtain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Curtain)

Two developments dominated the decade that followed: the increasingly apparent crumbling of the Soviet Union's economic and political structures, and the patchwork attempts at reforms to reverse that process. Kenneth S. Deffeyes argued in *[Beyond Oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beyond_Oil)* that the [Reagan administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Ronald_Reagan) encouraged [Saudi Arabia](349303.md) to [lower the price of oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980s_oil_glut) to the point where the Soviets could not make a profit selling their oil, and resulted in the depletion of the country's [hard currency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hard_currency) reserves.[^cite_note-367]

Brezhnev's next two successors, transitional figures with deep roots in his tradition, did not last long. [Yuri Andropov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuri_Andropov) was 68 years old and [Konstantin Chernenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konstantin_Chernenko) 72 when they assumed power; both died in less than two years. In an attempt to avoid a third short-lived leader, in 1985, the Soviets turned to the next generation and selected [Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev). He made significant changes in the economy and party leadership, called *[perestroika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perestroika)*. His policy of *[glasnost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasnost)* freed public [access to information](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_information) after decades of heavy government censorship. Gorbachev also moved to end the Cold War. In 1988, the USSR abandoned its [war in Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War) and began to [withdraw its forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_withdrawal_from_Afghanistan). In the following year, [Gorbachev refused to interfere in the internal affairs of the Soviet satellite states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinatra_Doctrine), which paved the way for the [Revolutions of 1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutions_of_1989). In particular, the standstill of the Soviet Union at the [Pan-European Picnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-European_Picnic) in August 1989 then set a peaceful chain reaction in motion, at the end of which the Eastern Bloc collapsed. With the tearing down of the [Berlin Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Berlin_Wall) and with East and West Germany pursuing re-unification, the [Iron Curtain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Curtain) between [the West](21208200.md) and Soviet-occupied regions came down.[^cite_note-Andreas_Rödder_2009-368][^cite_note-Thomas_Roser_2018-369][^cite_note-Otmar_Lahodynsky_2014-370]

By 1989, the Soviet alliance system was on the brink of collapse, and, deprived of Soviet military support, the communist leaders of the Warsaw Pact states were losing power.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005235–236-361] Grassroots organizations, such as Poland's [Solidarity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solidarity_\(Polish_trade_union\)) movement, rapidly gained ground with strong popular bases.



The [Pan-European Picnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-European_Picnic) took place in August 1989 on the Hungarian-Austrian border

The Pan-European Picnic in August 1989 in Hungary finally started a peaceful movement that the rulers in the Eastern Bloc could not stop. It was the largest movement of refugees from East Germany since the Berlin Wall was built in 1961 and ultimately brought about the fall of the Iron Curtain. The patrons of the picnic, [Otto von Habsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otto_von_Habsburg) and the Hungarian Minister of State [Imre Pozsgay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imre_Pozsgay), saw the planned event as an opportunity to test Mikhail Gorbachev's reaction. The Austrian branch of the [Paneuropean Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paneuropean_Union), which was then headed by Otto von Habsburg, distributed thousands of brochures inviting the GDR holidaymakers in Hungary to a picnic near the border at Sopron. But with the mass exodus at the Pan-European Picnic the subsequent hesitant behavior of the ruling Socialist Unity Party of East Germany and the non-interference of the Soviet Union broke the dams. Now tens of thousands of media-informed East Germans made their way to Hungary, which was no longer willing to keep its borders completely closed or to oblige its border troops to use armed force. On the one hand, this caused disagreement among the Eastern European states and, on the other hand, it was clear to the Eastern European population that the governments no longer had absolute power.[^cite_note-Andreas_Rödder_2009-368][^cite_note-Thomas_Roser_2018-369][^cite_note-Otmar_Lahodynsky_2014-370][^cite_note-371]



Romanian soldier giving the [victory sign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victory_sign) in the aftermath of the Romanian revolution

In 1989, the communist governments in Poland and Hungary became the first to negotiate the organization of competitive elections. In Czechoslovakia and East Germany, mass protests unseated entrenched communist leaders. The communist regimes in Bulgaria and Romania also crumbled, in the latter case as the result of a [violent uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_Revolution). Attitudes had changed enough that US Secretary of State [James Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Baker) suggested that the American government would not be opposed to Soviet intervention in Romania, on behalf of the opposition, to prevent bloodshed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarthoff1994\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_August_2024\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (August_2024)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-372]

The tidal wave of change culminated with the [fall of the Berlin Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Berlin_Wall) in November 1989, which symbolized the collapse of European communist governments and graphically ended the Iron Curtain divide of Europe. The [1989 revolutionary wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutions_of_1989) swept across Central and Eastern Europe and peacefully overthrew all of the Soviet-style [Marxist–Leninist states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_state): East Germany, Poland, Hungary, Czechoslovakia and Bulgaria;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELefeberFitzmauriceVierdag1991221-373] Romania was the only Eastern-bloc country to topple its communist regime violently and [execute its head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trial_and_execution_of_Nicolae_and_Elena_Ceau%C8%99escu).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005247-374]

### Soviet dissolution

Main article: [Dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union)

Further information: [History of the Soviet Union (1982–1991)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Soviet_Union_\(1982%E2%80%931991\)), [The Barricades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Barricades), [1991 Soviet coup d'état attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Soviet_coup_d'%C3%A9tat_attempt), [Commonwealth of Independent States](36870.md), [Economy of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_Soviet_Union), and [Baltic Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_Way)



The human chain in [Lithuania](17675.md) during the [Baltic Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_Way), 23 August 1989

At the same time, the Soviet republics started legal moves towards potentially declaring [sovereignty](28791.md) over their territories, citing the freedom to secede in Article 72 of the USSR constitution.[^cite_note-375] On 7 April 1990, a law was passed allowing a republic to secede if more than two-thirds of its residents voted for it in a referendum.[^cite_note-376] Many held their first free elections in the Soviet era for their own national legislatures in 1990. Many of these legislatures proceeded to produce legislation contradicting the Union laws in what was known as the '[War of Laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_Laws)'. In 1989, the [Russian SFSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic) convened a newly elected Congress of People's Deputies. [Boris Yeltsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Yeltsin) was elected its chairman. On 12 June 1990, the Congress [declared Russia's sovereignty over its territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_State_Sovereignty_of_the_Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic) and proceeded to pass laws that attempted to supersede some of the Soviet laws. After a landslide victory of [Sąjūdis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C4%85j%C5%ABdis) in Lithuania, that country declared its independence restored on 11 March 1990, citing the illegality of the [Soviet occupation of the Baltic states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_the_Baltic_states). Soviet forces attempted to halt the secession by crushing popular demonstrations in Lithuania ([Bloody Sunday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_Events)) and Latvia ([The Barricades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Barricades)), as a result, numerous civilians were killed or wounded. However, these actions only bolstered international support for the secessionists.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMälksoo2022\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_August_2024\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (August_2024)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-377]



[August Coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Soviet_coup_d'%C3%A9tat_attempt) in [Moscow](19004.md), 1991

A [referendum for the preservation of the USSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Soviet_Union_referendum) was held on 17 March 1991 in nine republics (the remainder having boycotted the vote), with the majority of the population in those republics voting for preservation of the Union in the form of a new federation. The referendum gave Gorbachev a minor boost. In the summer of 1991, the [New Union Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Union_Treaty), which would have turned the country into a much looser Union, was agreed upon by eight republics. The signing of the treaty, however, was interrupted by the [August Coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Soviet_coup_attempt)—an attempted coup d'état by hardline members of the government and the KGB who sought to reverse Gorbachev's reforms and reassert the central government's control over the republics. After the coup collapsed, Russian president Yeltsin was seen as a hero for his decisive actions, while Gorbachev's power was effectively ended. The balance of power tipped significantly towards the republics. In August 1991, Latvia and Estonia immediately declared the restoration of their full independence (following Lithuania's 1990 example). Gorbachev resigned as general secretary in late August, and soon afterwards, the party's activities were indefinitely suspended—effectively ending its rule. By the fall, Gorbachev could no longer influence events outside Moscow, and he was being challenged even there by Yeltsin, who had been elected [President of Russia](239646.md) in July 1991.



[T-80](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-80) tank on [Red Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Square) during the [August Coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Soviet_coup_attempt)



The first [Russian McDonald's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McDonald's_in_Russia) on [Moscow](19004.md)'s [Pushkin Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pushkinskaya_Square), pictured in 1991

Later in August, Gorbachev resigned as [general secretary of the Communist party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Secretary_of_the_Communist_Party_of_the_Soviet_Union), and [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic) President Boris Yeltsin ordered the seizure of Soviet property.
