Gorbachev clung to power as the President of the Soviet Union until 25 December 1991, when the [USSR dissolved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreene2015205–206-378] [Fifteen states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Soviet_states) emerged from the Soviet Union, with by far the largest and most populous one (which also was the founder of the Soviet state with the [October Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_Revolution) in Petrograd), the [Russian Federation](25391.md), taking full responsibility for all the rights and obligations of the USSR under the Charter of the United Nations, including the financial obligations. As such, Russia assumed the Soviet Union's [UN membership and permanent membership on the Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia_and_the_United_Nations), nuclear stockpile and the control over the armed forces.[^cite_note-web.archive.org-379]

In his [1992 State of the Union Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_State_of_the_Union_Address), US President George H. W. Bush expressed his emotions: "The biggest thing that has happened in the world in my life, in our lives, is this: By the grace of God, America won the Cold War."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmbroseBrinkley2011xvi-380] Bush and Yeltsin met in February 1992, declaring a new era of "friendship and partnership".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHanhimäkiSoutouGermond2010501-381] In January 1993, Bush and Yeltsin agreed to [START II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/START_II), which provided for further nuclear arms reductions on top of the original START treaty.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEvan_Dijk2013860–861-382]

## Aftermath

Main articles: [Effects of the Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_the_Cold_War), [International relations since 1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_relations_since_1989), [Post-Soviet states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Soviet_states), [Post-Soviet conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Soviet_conflicts), [Yugoslav Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoslav_Wars), [Second Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Cold_War), and [East–West dichotomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_dichotomy)



Changes in national boundaries after the end of the Cold War

In summing up the international ramifications of these events, [Vladislav Zubok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladislav_Zubok) stated: 'The collapse of the [Soviet empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_empire) was an event of epochal geopolitical, military, ideological, and economic significance.'[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZubok2009ix-383] After the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union), Russia drastically cut [military spending](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_military_expenditures), and restructuring the economy left millions unemployed.[^cite_note-384] According to Western analysis, the neoliberal reforms in Russia culminated in a [recession](25382.md) in the early 1990s more severe than the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) as experienced by the United States and Germany.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENolan199517–18-385] Western analysts suggest that in the 25 years following the end of the Cold War, only five or six of the post-communist states are on a path to joining the rich and capitalist world while most are falling behind, some to such an extent that it will take several decades to catch up to where they were before the collapse of communism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGhodsee201763-386][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMilanović2015135–138-387]

### Decommunization

[Stephen Holmes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Holmes_\(political_scientist\)) of the [University of Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Chicago) argued in 1996 that [decommunization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decommunization), after a brief active period, quickly ended in near-universal failure. After the introduction of [lustration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lustration), demand for scapegoats has become relatively low, and former communists have been elected for high governmental and other administrative positions. Holmes notes that the only real exception was former [East Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Germany), where thousands of former [Stasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stasi) informers have been fired from public positions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMandelbaum1996\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_August_2024\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (August_2024)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-388]

Holmes suggests the following reasons for the failure of decommunization:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMandelbaum1996\[\[Category\:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_August_2024\]\]<sup_class="noprint_Inline-Template_"_style="white-space\:nowrap;">\[<i>\[\[Wikipedia\:Citing_sources|<span_title="This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears. (August_2024)">page needed</span>\]\]</i>\]</sup>-388]

- After 45–70 years of communist rule, nearly every family has members associated with the state. After the initial desire "to root out the reds" came a realization that massive punishment is wrong and finding only some guilty is hardly justice.

- The urgency of the current economic problems of postcommunism makes the crimes of the communist past "old news" for many citizens.

- Decommunization is believed to be a power game of elites.

- The difficulty of dislodging the social elite makes it require a [totalitarian state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totalitarian_state) to disenfranchise the "[enemies of the people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enemies_of_the_people)" quickly and efficiently and a desire for normalcy overcomes the desire for punitive justice.

- Very few people have a perfectly clean slate and so are available to fill the positions that require significant expertise.

Compared with the decommunization efforts of the other former constituents of the [Eastern Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc) and the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union), decommunization in Russia has been restricted to half-measures, if conducted at all.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERyavec200313-389] Notable anti-communist measures in the Russian Federation include the banning of the [Communist Party of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_the_Soviet_Union) (and the creation of the [Communist Party of the Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_the_Russian_Federation)) as well as changing the names of some Russian cities back to what they were before the 1917 [October Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_Revolution) (Leningrad to [Saint Petersburg](24320051.md), Sverdlovsk to [Yekaterinburg](66524.md) and Gorky to [Nizhny Novgorod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nizhny_Novgorod)),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShevchenko2015-390] though others were maintained, with [Ulyanovsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulyanovsk) (former Simbirsk), [Tolyatti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tolyatti) (former Stavropol) and [Kirov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirov,_Kirov_Oblast) (former Vyatka) being examples. Even though Leningrad and Sverdlovsk were renamed, regions that were named after them are still officially called Leningrad and Sverdlovsk oblasts.[^cite_note-391]



The [Spasskaya Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spasskaya_Tower) had kept its red star and did not restore the two-headed eagle present before communist takeover

[Nostalgia for the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nostalgia_for_the_Soviet_Union) is gradually on the rise in Russia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERosenberg2016-392] Communist symbols continue to form an important part of the rhetoric used in [state-controlled media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Russia), as banning on them in other countries is seen by the [Russian foreign ministry](4432213.md) as "sacrilege" and "a perverse idea of good and evil".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShevchenko2015-390] The process of [decommunization in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decommunization_in_Ukraine), a neighbouring [post-Soviet state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Soviet_state), was met with fierce criticism by Russia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShevchenko2015-390] The [State Anthem of the Russian Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Anthem_of_the_Russian_Federation), adopted in 2000 (the same year [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) began his first term as president of Russia), uses the exact same music as the [State Anthem of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Anthem_of_the_Soviet_Union), but with new lyrics written by [Sergey Mikhalkov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Mikhalkov).[^cite_note-393][^cite_note-394]

Conversely, decommunization in Ukraine started during and after the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union) in 1991.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhotin2009-395] With the success of the [Revolution of Dignity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_Dignity) in 2014, the [Ukrainian government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_government) approved [laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_decommunization_laws) that outlawed [communist symbols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_symbol).[^cite_note-ectniiU-396] In July 2015, President of Ukraine [Petro Poroshenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petro_Poroshenko) signed a set of laws that started a six-month period for the removal of communist monuments (excluding [World War II](32927.md) monuments) and renaming of public places named after communist-related themes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShevchenko2015-390][^cite_note-397][^cite_note-398] At the time, this meant that 22 cities and 44 villages were set to get new names.[^cite_note-399] In 2016, 51,493 streets and 987 cities and villages were renamed, and 1,320 [Lenin monuments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lenin_monuments) and 1,069 monuments to other communist figures removed.[^cite_note-rdiU16-400] Violation of the law carries a penalty of a potential media ban and prison sentences of up to five years.[^cite_note-dwdc9415-401][^cite_note-oscedc18515-402] The [Ministry of the Interior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_the_Interior_\(Ukraine\)) stripped the [Communist Party of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Ukraine), the [Communist Party of Ukraine (renewed)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Ukraine_\(renewed\)), and the [Communist Party of Workers and Peasants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Workers_and_Peasants) of their right to participate in elections and stated it was continuing the court actions that started in July 2014 to end the registration of [communist parties in Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Communist_parties_in_Ukraine).[^cite_note-Banukcom24715-403] By 16 December 2015, these three parties had been banned in Ukraine; the Communist Party of Ukraine appealed the ban to the [European Court of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Court_of_Human_Rights).[^cite_note-404][^cite_note-405][^cite_note-406]

### Collapse of Yugoslavia and Balkan conflicts



NATO [psyop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psyop) flyer during the [Kosovo War](16760.md) 1999

[^cite_note-407]

The Cold War had provided external stabilizing pressures. Both the United States and the Soviet Union had a vested interest in Yugoslavia's stability, ensuring it remained a buffer state in the east–west divide. This resulted in financial and political support for its regime. When the Cold War ended, this external support evaporated, leaving Yugoslavia more vulnerable to internal divisions.[^cite_note-408][^cite_note-409]

As Yugoslavia fragmented, [the wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoslav_Wars) began after [Slovenia](27338.md) and [Croatia](5573.md) declared independence in 1991. [Serbia](29265.md), under [Slobodan Milošević](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slobodan_Milo%C5%A1evi%C4%87), opposed these moves.[^cite_note-410] [The Bosnian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosnian_War) (1992–1995) was the most brutal of the Yugoslav Wars, characterized by ethnic cleansing and genocide. International organizations, including the United Nations, struggled to manage the violence. NATO eventually intervened with airstrikes in Bosnia (1995) as part of [Operation Deliberate Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Deliberate_Force) and later in Kosovo (1999) as part of [Operation Allied Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_allied_force). These interventions marked the transition of NATO as a deterrent to the Soviet Union, to also functioning at the time as an active peacekeeping and conflict-resolution force.[^cite_note-411]

## Influence

The post-Cold War world is considered to be [unipolar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polarity_\(international_relations\)#Unipolarity), with the United States becoming the sole remaining [superpower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superpower).[^cite_note-412][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlum200687-413] The Cold War defined the political role of the United States after World War II—by 1989, the United States had military alliances with 50 countries, with 526,000 troops stationed abroad,[^cite_note-PBS_2004-414] with 326,000 in Europe (two-thirds of which were in [West Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Germany))[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuke1989175-415] and 130,000 in Asia (mainly [Japan](15573.md) and [South Korea](27019.md)).[^cite_note-PBS_2004-414] The Cold War also marked the zenith of peacetime [military–industrial complexes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military%E2%80%93industrial_complex) and large-scale [military funding of science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_military_technology).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2002-416]



Since the end of the Cold War, the [EU](9317.md) has [expanded eastwards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enlargement_of_the_European_Union) into the former Warsaw Pact and parts of the former Soviet Union

Cumulative US military expenditures throughout the entire Cold War amounted to an estimated \$8 trillion. Nearly 100,000 Americans died in the [Korean](16772.md) and [Vietnam Wars](32611.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELaFeber20021-417] Although Soviet casualties are difficult to estimate, as a share of gross national product the financial cost for the Soviet Union was much higher than that incurred by the United States.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005213-418]

Millions died in the superpowers' [proxy wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proxy_war) around the globe, most notably in eastern Asia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaddis2005266-419][^cite_note-420] Most of the proxy wars and subsidies for local conflicts ended along with the Cold War; interstate wars, ethnic wars, revolutionary wars, as well as refugee and displaced persons crises have declined sharply in the post-Cold War years.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarshallGurr2006-421]

Many of the economic and social tensions that were exploited to fuel Cold War competition in parts of the Third World remain acute to the present day. The breakdown of state control in a number of areas formerly ruled by communist governments produced new civil and ethnic conflicts, particularly in the former [Yugoslavia](34244.md). In Central and Eastern Europe, the end of the Cold War has ushered in an era of [economic growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_growth) and an increase in the number of [liberal democracies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_democracy), while in other parts of the world, such as Afghanistan, independence was accompanied by [state failure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Failed_state).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalliday20012e-290] It has been posited by several scholars that the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union) and the end of [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism) as a global force in the post-Cold War era allowed [neoliberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoliberal) [capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalism) to become the dominant global system, which has resulted in rising [economic inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_inequality).[^cite_note-422][^cite_note-423][^cite_note-424][^cite_note-425]

## In popular culture

See also: [The Cold War in popular culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Cold_War_in_popular_culture)

The Cold War endures as a popular topic reflected in entertainment media, and continuing to the present with Cold War-themed feature films, novels, television and web series, and other media.

## Historiography

Main article: [Historiography of the Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historiography_of_the_Cold_War)

Interpreting the course and origins of the conflict has been a source of heated controversy among historians, political scientists, and journalists.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENashel1999-426] In particular, historians have sharply disagreed as to who was responsible for the breakdown of Soviet–US relations after the Second World War; and whether the conflict between the two superpowers was inevitable or could have been avoided.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmbroseBrinkley2011789–799-427] Historians have also disagreed on what exactly the Cold War was, what the sources of the conflict were, and how to disentangle patterns of action and reaction between the two sides.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalliday20012e-290]

Although explanations of the origins of the conflict in academic discussions are complex and diverse, several general schools of thought on the subject can be identified. Historians commonly speak of three different approaches to the study of the Cold War: "orthodox" accounts, "revisionism", and "post-revisionism".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2002-416]

"Orthodox" accounts place responsibility for the Cold War on the Soviet Union and its expansion further into Europe.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2002-416] "Revisionist" writers place more responsibility for the breakdown of post-war peace on the United States, citing a range of US efforts to isolate and confront the Soviet Union well before the end of World War II.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2002-416] "Post-revisionists" see the events of the Cold War as more nuanced and attempt to be more balanced in determining what occurred during the Cold War.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECalhoun2002-416] Much of the historiography on the Cold War weaves together two or even all three of these broad categories.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEByrd2003-83]

[ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [9780598864048](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/9780598864048). `{{cite book}}`: ISBN / Date incompatibility ([help](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:CS1_errors#invalid_isbn_date))

[^cite_note-51]: &#x20;[Jowett & O'Donnell 2005](#CITEREFJowettO'Donnell2005), pp. 21–23: "In fact, the word disinformation is a cognate for the Russian dezinformatsia, taken from the name of a division of the KGB devoted to black propaganda."

[^cite_note-102]: &#x20;[Matray 2002](#CITEREFMatray2002): "South Korea's President Rhee was obsessed with accomplishing early reunification through military means. The Truman administration's fear that Rhee would launch an invasion prompted it to limit South Korea's military capabilities, refusing to provide tanks, heavy artillery, and combat planes. This did not stop the South Koreans from initiating most of the border clashes with North Korean forces at the thirty-eighth parallel beginning in the summer of 1948 and reaching a high level of intensity and violence a year later. Historians now acknowledge that the two Koreas already were waging a civil conflict when North Korea's attack opened the conventional phase of the war."

[^cite_note-105]: &#x20;[Matray 2002](#CITEREFMatray2002): "Contradicting traditional assumptions, however, available declassified Soviet documents demonstrate that throughout 1949 Stalin consistently refused to approve Kim Il Sung's persistent requests to approve an invasion of South Korea. The Soviet leader believed that North Korea had not achieved either military superiority north of the parallel or political strength south of that line. His main concern was the threat South Korea posed to North Korea's survival, for example fearing an invasion northward following U.S. military withdrawal in June 1949."

[^cite_note-123]: &#x20;["Revolt in Hungary"](https://web.archive.org/web/20071117094223/http://files.osa.ceu.hu/holdings/selection/rip/4/av/1956-44.html). Archived from [the original](http://files.osa.ceu.hu/holdings/selection/rip/4/av/1956-44.html) on 17 November 2007. Narrator: [Walter Cronkite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Cronkite), producer: CBS (1956) – Fonds 306, Audiovisual Materials Relating to the 1956 Hungarian Revolution, OSA Archivum, Budapest, Hungary ID number: HU OSA 306-0-1\:40

[^cite_note-178]: &#x20;34,374,483 square kilometres (13,272,062 sq mi).

[^cite_note-206]: &#x20;[Prados & Jimenez-Bacardi 2019](#CITEREFPradosJimenez-Bacardi2019): "The memorandum showed no concern for international law or the unspoken nature of these operations as terrorist attacks."

[^cite_note-207]: &#x20;International Policy Report (Report). [Washington, D.C.](108956.md): [Center for International Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_International_Policy). 1977. pp. 10–12. To coordinate and carry out its war of terror and destruction during the early 1960s, the CIA established a base of operations, known as [JMWAVE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JMWAVE).

[^cite_note-223]: &#x20;National Research Council Committee on Antarctic Policy and Science, p. 33

[^cite_note-229]: &#x20;[Robinson 2018](#CITEREFRobinson2018), p. 203: "A US Embassy official in Jakarta, Robert Martens, had supplied the Indonesian Army with lists containing the names of thousands of PKI officials in the months after the alleged coup attempt. According to the journalist Kathy Kadane, "As many as 5,000 names were furnished over a period of months to the Army there, and the Americans later checked off the names of those who had been killed or captured." Despite Martens later denials of any such intent, these actions almost certainly aided in the death or detention of many innocent people. They also sent a powerful message that the US government agreed with and supported the army's campaign against the PKI, even as that campaign took its terrible toll in human lives."

[^cite_note-230]: &#x20;[Simpson 2010](#CITEREFSimpson2010), p. 193: "Washington did everything in its power to encourage and facilitate the army-led massacre of alleged PKI members, and U.S. officials worried only that the killing of the party's unarmed supporters might not go far enough, permitting Sukarno to return to power and frustrate the \[Johnson] Administration's emerging plans for a post-Sukarno Indonesia. This was efficacious terror, an essential building block of the [neoliberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoliberal) policies that the West would attempt to impose on Indonesia after Sukarno's ouster."

[^cite_note-302]: &#x20;[Coll 2004](#CITEREFColl2004), pp. 47–49: "Frustrated and hoping to discredit him, the KGB initially planted false stories that Amin was a CIA agent. In the autumn these rumors rebounded on the KGB in a strange case of "[blowback](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blowback_\(intelligence\))," the term used by spies to describe planted propaganda that filters back to confuse the country that first set the story loose."

[^cite_note-303]: &#x20;[Jones, S. 2010](#CITEREFJones,_S.2010), pp. [16](https://archive.org/details/ingraveyardofemp00jone_0/page/16)–17: "'It was total nonsense,' said the CIA's [Graham Fuller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graham_E._Fuller). 'I would have been thrilled to have those kinds of contacts with Amin, but they didn't exist.'"

[^cite_note-338]: &#x20;"[Official Energy Statistics of the US Government](http://www.eia.doe.gov/emeu/international/contents.html)", EIA – International Energy Data and Analysis. Retrieved on 4 July 2008.

[^cite_note-420]: &#x20;[Kim 2014](#CITEREFKim2014), p. 45: "With three of the four major Cold War fault lines—divided Germany, divided Korea, divided China, and divided Vietnam—East Asia acquired the dubious distinction of having engendered the largest number of armed conflicts resulting in higher fatalities between 1945 and 1994 than any other region or sub-region. Even in Asia, while Central and South Asia produced a regional total of 2.8 million in human fatalities, East Asia's regional total is 10.4 million including the Chinese Civil War (1 million), the Korean War (3 million), the Vietnam War (2 million), and the Pol Pot genocide in Cambodia (1 to 2 million)."

[^cite_note-422]: &#x20;[Ghodsee, Kristen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kristen_Ghodsee) (2018). *[Why Women Have Better Sex Under Socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Why_Women_Have_Better_Sex_Under_Socialism)*. [^cite_note-424]: &#x20;Bartel, Fritz (2022). [*The Triumph of Broken Promises: The End of the Cold War and the Rise of Neoliberalism*](https://www.hup.harvard.edu/catalog.php?isbn=9780674976788). [Harvard University Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard_University_Press). pp. 5–6. [^cite_note-425]: &#x20;[Gerstle, Gary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Gerstle) (2022). [*The Rise and Fall of the Neoliberal Order: America and the World in the Free Market Era*](https://global.oup.com/academic/product/the-rise-and-fall-of-the-neoliberal-order-9780197519646?cc=us\&lang=en&). [Oxford University Press](48518.md). pp. 10–12, 149. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_\(identifier\)) [978-0-19-751964-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:BookSources/978-0-19-751964-6). [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220626220259/https://global.oup.com/academic/product/the-rise-and-fall-of-the-neoliberal-order-9780197519646?cc=us\&lang=en&) from the original on 26 June 2022. Retrieved 23 June 2025. The collapse of communism, then, opened the entire world to capitalist penetration, shrank the imaginative and ideological space in which opposition to capitalist thought and practices might incubate, and impelled those who remained leftists to redefine their radicalism in alternative terms, which turned out to be those that capitalist systems could more, rather than less, easily manage. This was the moment when neoliberalism in the United States went from being a political movement to a political order.
